Nothing in the American inventory of 1964 had needed coordinates of that kind before, and nobody in the theater knew how to make them; Sorrell trained his first twenty analysts himself, on imagery of Tan Son Nhut and the Saigon bridges, against points his survey teams had fixed with receivers on the ground²⁴.

The cell's first discovery was an error in every map in the country. The Army Map Service's 1:50,000 sheets of Indochina were drawn on the Indian 1960 datum, and the U-2S's coordinates were true to the satellites; at Tan Son Nhut the two differed by several hundred metres, and in the mountains of the panhandle, where the sheets themselves were drawn from French surveys of the 1930s and air photographs, the map placed some ridges and river crossings 300 metres or more from where they stood²⁵. Sorrell's memorandum of 14 May 1964 to the MACV J-2 and the Seventh Air Force's predecessor, the 2nd Air Division, said in one sentence what the cell would spend a decade enforcing: "No coordinate taken from a map is to be given to a guided weapon, and no coordinate given to a guided weapon is to be plotted on a map without conversion." The Army's artillery in the South took a second lesson from the same memorandum: by the end of 1965 the cell had given the II Field Force and the 1st Cavalry Division survey control points at every major base, and its first artillery batteries were laid on true coordinates²⁴.

By June 1965 the cell held about 1,400 folders: every road and rail bridge between the 20th parallel and the DMZ, the ferries on the Song Ca, Song Giang and Kien Giang, the passes at Mu Gia and Ban Karai, the fuel and ammunition depots around Vinh and Dong Hoi, and the first truck parks the U-2S found in the Laotian panhandle. About a quarter to a third of the U-2S's hours went to re-imaging known targets for the folders rather than searching for new ones, which is a cost this command paid knowingly²⁵. On Guam the 3960th Strategic Wing received its first B-52Js with GBU-31 and GBU-38 kits in storage from the spring of 1964, and the crews trained on ranges in the Marianas against coordinates the cell sent them²⁴. Through all of 1964 and the first half of 1965 no folder was struck. The aircraft were ready; the authority was not. That interval, sixteen months of a precision bombing force with a target list and nothing to do, is the whole of the provision's history in this phase, and its consequence is in the next.

#### The first titling teams
*16 Mar – 31 Dec 1964 · An Giang, Phong Dinh, Vinh Long, Gia Dinh, Bien Hoa and Long An Provinces, and the coastal Centre from Quang Nam to Binh Dinh · new · EV001-2*
*Initiative: allied*

The first 40 mobile titling teams of about 25 men each took the field on 16 March 1964, working outward from province capitals and main roads into villages the district could reinforce by day, each escorted by a Regional Forces company armed with M1 and M2 carbines and M1918A2 automatic rifles. The first titles were handed over at Long Xuyen in An Giang on 21 April, in the Hoa Hao country where the Front was weakest, and the first in a contested district at Phung Hiep in Phong Dinh on 11 May. By July 70 teams were in the field, the most the provinces could escort without stripping their daytime reaction forces. They moved by road, in the LCM-6 and LCVP craft of the River Assault Groups and by hired sampan, and to villages beyond both by CH-21C and UH-1B helicopters when the operations staff would release them².

By 31 December 1964 titles had been issued for about 210,000 hectares to about 170,000 families, about a fifth of the target, almost all of it in hamlets held by the government at night or contested only lightly: An Giang, the riverine districts of Phong Dinh and Vinh Long along the Bassac, the inner districts of Gia Dinh and Bien Hoa, and the coastal plain around Hoi An and Tam Ky in Quang Nam. The rent stop held at the main-season harvest of December 1964 in most districts the government controlled; landlords' agents collected under Regional Forces escort in parts of Vinh Long, Vinh Binh and Dinh Tuong, which led to the first suspension a few months later. Twenty-three team members were killed by mines and ambush during the year². The figure the Mission reported to Washington each month was titles issued; this headquarters reported it with the warning that it measured the government's paperwork, not the peasants' allegiance.

#### The dawn musters
*7 Apr – 31 Dec 1964 · ARVN 7th, 9th and 21st Divisions and the Regional and Popular Forces of the Delta, then countrywide · new · EV001-4*
*Initiative: allied*

The Pay and Muster Verification Service's first 20 teams began unannounced dawn musters of the ARVN 21st Division's 31st, 32nd and 33rd Regiments around Bac Lieu and Soc Trang on 7 April 1964, and of the 7th and 9th Divisions in Dinh Tuong and Vinh Long in May; by the autumn 60 teams were mustering about 480 units a month across all four corps. Each man was enrolled on an identity card with his photograph and fingerprints, and from August the Service's paymasters paid the enrolled units of IV Corps at the parade rather than through their commanders. By 31 December about 41,000 names had been struck from the rolls of a force carried at about 514,000, about 8%; the rate ran from about 4% in the Airborne and Marine battalions to about 15% in some Regional Forces companies of the Delta². These are counts of men present against names on the roll, not estimates, and they are the best figures this headquarters had ever had for the army it advised.

Washington read them as a smaller army. Secretary McNamara, visiting in May, was briefed on the first returns and told the President that the RVNAF's real strength was below its reported strength by a margin that would grow as the musters spread. In the units mustered twice, the second muster found few ghosts, because commanders had struck them before the teams came. The Commission opened 412 cases by the end of the year, almost all against company and battalion commanders and province finance officers, and brought 61 to trial².

#### The Can Giuoc killings
*9 Jun – 14 Jul 1964 · Can Giuoc and Can Duoc Districts, Long An Province · new · EV001-3*
*Initiative: enemy*

In May 1964 titling teams from Tan An began work in the villages of Can Giuoc and Can Duoc, the rice districts of Long An between Saigon and the sea, where the survey of December 1963 had found most of the strategic hamlets destroyed and the Front's infrastructure intact. The province chief, under pressure from Saigon for a showcase close to the capital, sent the teams into hamlets the government held by day only and posted the claimants' names in the village offices, as the procedure then required². Each team worked under the escort of a Long An Regional Forces company that withdrew to its post at dusk.

On the night of 9 June armed propaganda teams of the Can Giuoc district committee, with the Long An provincial security section and screened by a company of the provincial 506th Local Force Battalion, entered three hamlets of Phuoc Lai village, read out names from the posted list, and shot four men who had claimed titles and the village land committee's secretary. Over the following five weeks the same pattern was repeated in eleven hamlets of the two districts: by 14 July 37 title holders and 9 members of village land committees had been killed, and several hundred families had brought their titles back to the district office at Can Giuoc rather than keep them in the house. The Regional Forces companies, posted in mud forts along Route 5 and the canal, did not move at night, and no reaction force came. The figures are this headquarters' count from district and province reports, cross-checked by the American province adviser, and are a floor².

The killings were the land programme's first lesson in what it did not change. Teams stopped posting names in hamlets not held at night, and titles in contested hamlets were thereafter held at the district office until the hamlet was secured or the owner asked for his. A COSVN directive captured near Duc Hoa in August told district committees not to obstruct the titling, to let the peasants "take the puppets' paper", to keep collecting the Front's agricultural tax, and to make examples only where a hamlet's people began to inform². In Can Giuoc the titling was suspended until the following spring. It was the first evidence that the reform's value in any district was exactly the value of the security behind it.

#### ◆ The camps, the bases and the towns: Nam Dong, Bien Hoa and Song Be
*6 Jul 1964 – 12 May 1965 · Nam Dong CIDG camp west of Da Nang; Bien Hoa air base; Song Be and Phuoc Binh, Phuoc Long Province · changed from history · EVCP01-3*
*Initiative: enemy*

Three attacks are told together because they showed one thing three ways: the enemy chose the night and the target, and what held was whatever was already inside the wire.

At 02:30 on 6 July 1964 about two battalions of VC and PAVN, some 800–900 men, attacked the CIDG camp at Nam Dong near the Laotian border, held by Special Forces Detachment A-726 under Captain Roger H. C. Donlon with an Australian warrant officer, 311 Montagnard strikers, 50 Nungs and seven Vietnamese Special Forces. The outer perimeter was penetrated and many strikers deserted or went over; the inner perimeter, held by the Americans and the Nungs, survived five hours of close fighting until the attackers withdrew at dawn. Two Americans and the Australian were killed, 58 strikers and Nungs died, more than 100 strikers defected, and 55–62 attackers' bodies lay inside the wire; Donlon became the first American awarded the Medal of Honor in Vietnam²⁶. The strikers carried the weapons they had always carried, because the rearmament did not reach the CIDG.

On the night of 31 October–1 November, two days before the American election, a VC mortar team fired on Bien Hoa air base for about thirty minutes, destroying five of the B-57Bs parked wingtip to wingtip there since the Tonkin crisis and destroying or damaging as many as 30 aircraft in all; four Americans were killed and 72 people wounded, and the attackers escaped²⁷. In this war an 81mm round burst on the revetment of the U-2S that was in maintenance that night and holed its left wing, fuel cells and forward equipment bay. It was out of service from 1 November to 20 December, and the daily deep look over the panhandle, where the dry season's first trucks were moving, lapsed to twice a week¹⁸. I asked for and got a covered shelter for each U-2S, built by the Seabees by February 1965, and a Regional Forces company for the hamlets on the base's approaches¹⁹. Johnson declined a reprisal on the eve of the election, and the question of who would guard the bases brought the first American combat troops ashore four months later²⁸.

On 10–11 May 1965 B-2 Front forces, the 763rd Regiment with the 840th Battalion or elements of the 271st–273rd Regiments, attacked Song Be, the capital of Phuoc Long, and the district town of Phuoc Binh. The 36th Ranger Battalion, about 150 territorials and about three dozen American advisers of Detachment B-34 held the town with the gunships of the 197th Aviation Company, and a relieving Ranger battalion arrived by midday on 11 May. The ARVN lost 58 killed, 96 wounded and 83 missing, five Americans were killed and a B-57 was lost; MACV's estimate of up to 1,000 enemy killed and wounded is of the kind A13 warns against²⁹. The Rangers had carried the M4A1 and the M249 since February 1964. I do not adjust the losses, most of which were taken in the first hour to mortars, recoilless rifles and sappers inside the wire, where the squad weapons counted for little³⁰.

In this war a fourth thing stood over each of the three nights, and it changed what was inside the wire at Nam Dong and nothing at Song Be. At Nam Dong a Ghostrider of the 16th Squadron from Da Nang, 50 kilometres away, was over the camp at 0305, thirty-five minutes after the first mortar rounds, in clear sky. The attackers who had broken through the outer perimeter were by then among the strikers' huts and could not be fired on, but the assault companies still forming in the treeline to the west and the mortar positions on the hill to the north could, and the 105mm and 30mm worked them for two and a half hours, until the attack broke off at about 0530 rather than at dawn. Donlon's inner perimeter held as it held historically. I put the strikers' and Nungs' dead at about 40 rather than 58, the two Americans and the Australian killed as historically because they fell in the first hour, and the attackers' dead at about 90–120, the video counting about 70 in the treeline. At Bien Hoa on 1 November the mortar team's rounds fell among the gunships as well as the B-57s: two Ghostriders of the 4th Squadron were hit on the ramp, one destroyed by fire and one out of service for six weeks, and from that night this headquarters asked for, and in March 1965 received with the Marines' and the Koreans' arrival, a ground force to hold the approaches to every gunship base. At Song Be the Ghostriders were over the town from 0115 on 11 May and fired through the night, but the losses had been taken in the first hour inside the wire, to mortars, recoilless rifles and sappers already among the defenders, where no aircraft could fire; the gunships broke the second assault at about 0400 and covered the Ranger battalion's landing at first light, and I do not adjust the allied losses²².

#### The battle of Nam Dong
*6 Jul 1964 · Nam Dong CIDG camp, about 50 km west of Da Nang near the Laotian border · no longer happens · EV000-2*
*Consolidated at CP01 into EVCP01-3; the event still happens.*

#### Operation DRY SEASON: the Can Giuoc security section
*20 Jul – 18 Dec 1964 · Can Giuoc, Can Duoc and Rach Kien Districts, Long An Province; the Rung Sat edge of Nha Be, Gia Dinh Province · new · EV012-2*
*Initiative: allied*

The task force's first campaign was aimed at the men who had killed the title holders of Phuoc Lai. The Long An provincial security section and the Can Giuoc district committee's armed propaganda teams had killed 37 title holders and 9 land committee members between 9 June and 14 July, and the province had suspended titling in the two districts². On 20 July the task force's III Corps task group, then about 180 strong, opened Operation DRY SEASON from a compound at Tan An under an American Special Forces major, Thomas R. Garvin, with a Special Branch captain, Nguyen Huu Phuoc, as his deputy¹⁶. Its raid element was one Special Forces detachment, a SEAL platoon of SEAL Team One on loan from the Rung Sat, and the Long An Provincial Reconnaissance Unit of about 70 men. Its lift was three UH-1Bs and two UH-1Ds of the 120th Aviation Company, kept on call at Tan Son Nhut.

The first two months produced almost nothing. The task group had the Long An Special Branch's files, six months old and built on hamlets the police held by day only. The district centre at Can Giuoc did not yet exist. Of 19 operations in August and September, 14 found the target gone, 3 took a man the magistrate at Tan An released within 72 hours, and 2 took real cadres who knew nothing of the security section. The 2026 cycle was attempted and abandoned in the same weeks. The task group's American planners ran raids two and three nights running on targets the exploitation cell had not confirmed, and the magistrate released 11 of 17 detainees in September¹⁶.

The turn came from a ledger. On 3 October a Rach Kien PF platoon ambushed a tax team on the canal at Long Huu and killed two men. One of them carried a notebook listing the district committee's monthly collections by hamlet, with a courier's route to the security section's base in the nipa swamps on the Nha Be edge of the Rung Sat. The exploitation cell had the notebook translated by morning. On the night of 5–6 October the SEAL platoon went up the Soai Rap in two STAB boats and a PBR borrowed from the Vietnamese Navy's river assault group. It inserted at the mouth of the Rach Ong Chuong and walked into the courier station at 0340. It took four men alive and a fifth was killed reaching for a K-50. One of the four was the security section's deputy chief, Ba Lien, who had commanded the Phuoc Lai killings. He was handed to Phuoc at Tan An at 0710 and before a magistrate on 7 October¹⁶.

What Ba Lien told the interrogators, and what his papers told the exploitation cell, opened the section. Over the following ten weeks the task group mounted 41 operations in Can Giuoc, Can Duoc, Rach Kien and the Nha Be edge. They took 58 people on warrant, of whom the magistrates released 19, and killed 9, two of them men whom the Commission's audit in January could not show to be cadres. Of the security section's 23 members, 14 were dead, in prison or had rallied by 18 December. Its chief had gone to the Rung Sat proper, where the task group could not follow without a battalion¹⁶. One PRU member and one SEAL were killed, the SEAL by a booby-trapped bunker at Ap Dong Thanh on 2 November.

Titling resumed in the eight Can Giuoc hamlets with PF posts in February 1965, two months earlier than on the course of memorandum 001. The other hamlets waited. The section was rebuilt by the spring of 1965 from cadres sent down from the Saigon–Gia Dinh Region, but the new men did not know the hamlets, and no title holder was killed in Can Giuoc in 1965¹⁶. DRY SEASON was a small operation that showed two things: the task force was worth nothing in its first two months, and it was worth a security section once it held one notebook.

#### The Gulf of Tonkin incidents and the Southeast Asia Resolution
*2–11 Aug 1964 · Gulf of Tonkin and Washington · changed from history · EV000-3*
*Initiative: both*

On 30–31 July 1964 South Vietnamese commando boats under the covert OPLAN 34A programme, run by MACV's Studies and Observations Group, raided the North Vietnamese islands of Hon Me and Hon Ngu. On 2 August the destroyer USS *Maddox*, on a DESOTO signals intelligence patrol under Captain John J. Herrick, was attacked in daylight by three P-4 torpedo boats of the 135th Torpedo Squadron. The *Maddox* fired, four F-8 Crusaders from USS *Ticonderoga* attacked the retiring boats, and the North Vietnamese lost four killed and six wounded with three boats damaged; the *Maddox* was struck by one machine-gun round and suffered no casualties. Johnson, as historically, chose a warning rather than retaliation, and ordered the patrol resumed with USS *Turner Joy* in company³¹.

At Admiral Sharp's request I moved the Global Hawk orbit from the II Corps coast to the Gulf on 3 August. There were no SA-2s in the North and nothing that could reach 60,000 feet, and the aircraft took station about 40 nautical miles east of Hon Me, with the destroyers, the coast from Hon Matt to the mouth of the Song Ma and the open Gulf to the east in its radar's maritime mode¹⁸. On the night of 4 August the *Maddox* and *Turner Joy*, in rough weather with thunderstorms, fired for about two hours at radar contacts they took to be a second attack. The watch in the exploitation cell at Tan Son Nhut that night was Major Harold Kessling's. His radar log showed the two destroyers and their manoeuvres, a cluster of fishing junks about 30 nautical miles to the west, rain cells moving across the Gulf, and no fast small craft within 40 nautical miles of the ships at any time between 2000 and 2330 local. P-4 boats making attack runs at 40 knots are good radar targets even in a sea state of three and four, and the radar had tracked the three boats of 2 August from their base on the same set. Herrick cabled that the "entire action leaves many doubts"³¹. Kessling's report reached MACV at about midnight Saigon time and CINCPAC an hour later, which in Washington was the late morning of 4 August, while McNamara and Sharp were still trying to establish what had happened¹⁹.

What followed was Washington's decision, and it went the other way from history. Historically Johnson had decided on retaliation within thirty minutes of the first reports³¹. Here McNamara told him in the early afternoon that the ships' reports were contradicted by a radar that had watched the whole engagement, that Herrick himself doubted them, and that the Navy could not confirm an attack. Johnson held the strike order and, at about 1900, cancelled it. Operation Pierce Arrow was not flown. The 64 sorties against the torpedo-boat bases and the Vinh oil storage did not happen; Lieutenant (jg) Richard Sather was not killed over Hon Gai, and Lieutenant (jg) Everett Alvarez was not shot down and taken prisoner that day¹⁹.

The resolution was sent anyway. Johnson had carried a draft since May, an election was three months away, and the attack of 2 August was real. On 5 August he asked Congress for authority to repel armed attack against US forces and to prevent further aggression, citing the 2 August attack on a US destroyer in international waters and Hanoi's support of the war in the South, and he said nothing of the night of the 4th. The debate ran three days longer than history's: Senator Wayne Morse, tipped about the 34A raids, asked why the destroyer had been near Hon Me at all, and Senator Gaylord Nelson's amendment against a land war was again declined by Senator Fulbright on the administration's assurance that no such war was intended. Congress passed the Southeast Asia Resolution on 10 August, 414–0 in the House and 85–3 in the Senate, in the words of the historical text¹⁹. Johnson signed it on 11 August. It was again the only legislative authority for the war that followed, but it rested on one attack that had happened rather than on two of which one had not, and the radar log of 4 August sat in the files at Tan Son Nhut and Honolulu as a record of a false alarm, not as the concealed proof of a pretext. B-57B bombers were moved to Bien Hoa in the aftermath²⁷, within mortar range of the countryside around it.

#### MACV-JSOC stands up and Delta arrives
*15 Aug – 31 Dec 1964 · Tan Son Nhut, Saigon; forward operating bases at Da Nang, Kontum, Bien Hoa and Can Tho · new · EV014-1*
*Initiative: allied*

COMUSMACV's order of 15 August 1964 created the Joint Special Operations Command, Vietnam, eleven days after the second report from the Gulf of Tonkin that this war never answered with Pierce Arrow. Brigadier General Harold M. Vance, lately of the Special Warfare Center at Fort Bragg, took it with a staff that reached 280 by December and 390 by the following June: a joint targeting cell, a joint operations centre that stood a watch from 18:00 to 06:00, and nineteen liaison cells — MACV J-2 and J-3, MACV-SOG, the CIA station, the Combined Interagency Task Force and its National Fusion Centre, the combined command's operations centre, the 2nd Air Division, the four US corps-level commands and the four ARVN corps headquarters, the Joint General Staff, the National Police, the US Mission and the Embassy in Vientiane³². The headquarters held no authority anyone else had held: it could command the units assigned to it, declare a restricted operating area over a hamlet for a night, and claim a target from the task force's deck. It could compel nothing outside MACV.

The 1st Special Forces Operational Detachment-Delta came in three lifts of MATS C-135Bs and C-124s from Pope Air Force Base between 28 August and 12 September 1964, 1,310 officers and men under Colonel James E. Teague, with 288 operators in four assault squadrons and G Squadron; E Squadron's unmarked light helicopters came by sea to Saigon port on 24 September and flew from Tan Son Nhut from 2 October³². Three squadrons went forward to Bien Hoa, Can Tho and Da Nang and one stayed at Tan Son Nhut to rest and retrain, which is the rotation the unit kept for the next eight years and the reason its sustained rate was never what its first month suggested.

The first three months were learning and they were cheap. Thirty-one raids were mounted in the Saigon belt and the upper Delta between 11 October and 31 December, all of them on addresses from the task force's Gia Dinh and Long An files; nine reached a cadre the file had listed at district level or above, and nineteen found the house empty or the wrong man in it. Teague's men did not know the country: the Delta's canal lines, the way a hamlet's dogs work, which path a cadre takes when he runs. On 14 and 22 November the squadron at Bien Hoa tried the concept it had brought with it, a Delta troop as the entry force with a company of the 32nd Ranger Battalion as the cordon, at Phu Hoa Dong and at Trung Lap. Both times the cordon moved into position before midnight and both times the house was empty by the time the troop reached it, and on the second the Rangers' battalion had been given the hamlet's name the previous afternoon. The concept was abandoned in December and nothing replaced it: from then on Delta went in without a cordon and accepted that some of the men in the house would get into the dark. The unit's first dead was Sergeant First Class Donald R. Keel, shot through the throat at a doorway in Can Giuoc on 2 December 1964; two more operators were wounded in the same ten weeks³².

#### Saigon's autumn of 1964: the Vung Tau Charter, the September coup and Hop Tac
*16 Aug 1964 – mid-1965 · Saigon, Hue and Da Nang; the Hop Tac ring of Gia Dinh, Bien Hoa and Long An Provinces · changed from history · EVCP02-1*
*Initiative: neither*

The autumn of 1964 is told here as one event, because the coup that failed in September is what left Hop Tac without its battalions. Khanh tried to use the Tonkin crisis to make himself president under the Vung Tau Charter of 16 August 1964; students and the Buddhist movement led by Thich Tri Quang answered with riots in Saigon, Hue and Da Nang, and the charter was repealed. The Military Revolutionary Council of 26–27 August could agree on no successor, and a triumvirate of Khanh, Minh and Khiem, dominated by Khanh, was formed; Khiem was sent to Washington as ambassador and Minh on a "goodwill tour". Before dawn on 13 September Generals Lam Van Phat and Duong Van Duc, with about ten battalions, four of them up from the Delta, seized key points in Saigon while Khanh took refuge in Da Lat. US officials backed Khanh, Ky flew jets low over the city and brought loyal marines and paratroopers into Tan Son Nhut, Thi supported him, and the plotters backed down on 14 September without a death. The lasting result was the Young Turks around Ky and Thi, who had saved Khanh and knew they could unmake him³³.

In this war the coup-makers had a programme as well as grievances. Duc had learned in August that the Commission had opened a case on the ghost payrolls the musters had found in IV Corps, and Phat's broadcast promised to "suspend the land decree pending review" and to dissolve the "foreign-controlled" Aid Compliance Board; it also promised to "restore the authority of the corps commanders over the provinces", which meant abolishing the Selection Board. It was the first coup to announce a policy toward the countryside, and the policy was the landlords'. The Special Branch had reported to Cuong on 11 September that battalions of the 7th Division were being readied at My Tho without orders, and Cuong passed the report to Khanh and the US Mission that night; on 13 September the Saigon police stayed in their stations and the Field Force companies in their barracks on his order, and Ambassador Taylor told the plotters within hours that the United States would not recognise them. The coup's failure attached the reforms to the side that won: the Young Turks took up the land decree and the Board as their own causes, and Ky spoke of the decree in his first public statements as an argument for their claim to govern²³.

In September, at the urging of the Mission and Westmoreland, the government launched Hop Tac, the "oil spot" applied to a ring around the capital, with the explicit priority of Saigon over everywhere else. Taylor's mission reported on 6 October that the provinces rated unsatisfactory had risen from 7 in July to 14 in August, named the diversion to Hop Tac as a possible cause, and recommended unanimously that it be given full support³⁴. Its ARVN units were pulled away by every coup and coup alert, its chain of command changed with each government, and by mid-1965 it was moribund, the infrastructure of Gia Dinh, Long An and the provinces north of the capital largely intact.

Here Hop Tac carried the titling teams into the inner ring of Gia Dinh and Bien Hoa, where about 38,000 hectares were titled by mid-1965, fewer families paid the Front's collectors without coercion, and volunteered reports rose from a trickle to a few dozen a month². Gia Dinh, Bien Hoa and Long An received province chiefs from the list between May and July 1964, and about 160 police posts were settled in the inner districts by mid-1965, with a Special Branch section in each district town. The Special Branch broke two of the Front's village committees in Gia Dinh and the courier line from the Hoc Mon rice fields into the city's northern wards, and the hamlets around the posts were held at night through the coups even when Hop Tac's battalions were pulled away³. That did not save the programme, whose failure foreshadowed the war's: pacification needed a stable government and a force that stayed in the hamlets at night, and in 1964 the Republic had only the beginning of the second.

#### Hanoi commits the regulars; the Trail and the first air war in Laos
*Sep 1964 – Apr 1965 · North Vietnam, the Laotian panhandle and the Central Highlands · changed from history · EV000-6*
*Initiative: enemy*

The Ninth Plenum of the Lao Dong Party Central Committee, in December 1963, had resolved to seek a decisive military victory in the South before the United States could intervene in strength. Through 1964 the Politburo under Le Duan acted on it. Infiltration down the Trail, managed since 1959 by the PAVN's Group 559, shifted from regrouped southerners to northern-born conscripts, and in the autumn of 1964 the first complete PAVN regular regiments were ordered south. The 95th Regiment of the 325th Division arrived in Kontum Province in December 1964; the 101st Regiment followed in February 1965 with the division commander, Colonel Nguyen Huu An; the 18th Regiment came a few months later. The division was then broken up among the B3 (Central Highlands) Front and Military Region 5³⁵. These were the first regular North Vietnamese formations committed to the war, and by the US estimate of 1965 about 4,500 PAVN troops and 300 tons of materiel moved through Laos each month³⁶. Both figures are intelligence estimates of their time; the movement was disciplined, at night, under canopy and through a country that US ground forces could not enter.

The decision to commit the regulars had been taken before the land decree existed, and it was not caused by it. The decree gave it a second argument. Party reporting from the Delta through 1964 described the "land trick" as the most dangerous of the enemy's measures, because it threatened the movement's base in exactly the provinces where it was strongest, and Le Duan's line that victory must come before the Americans and before the political base eroded drew strength from it. The schedule of the 325th Division's regiments was set by their training and by the Trail, not by the reform, and the regiments came when they would have come².

The American answer in Laos began on 14 December 1964 with Operation Barrel Roll, a small programme of armed reconnaissance by US aircraft over northern and eastern Laos. On 3 April 1965 the 2nd Air Division opened Operation Steel Tiger against the Trail in the southeastern panhandle³⁶. Both were bound by the fiction of Laotian neutrality under the Geneva Accords of 1962, by the ambassador in Vientiane's control of targets, and by the bar on ground operations across the border. The Trail at this stage was still largely foot and bicycle traffic with truck routes being built; its capacity was growing faster than anything flying over it could reduce.

#### The trawlers turn back; the Sihanoukville route opens
*Sep 1964 – Jun 1965 · Haiphong and the Gulf of Tonkin; the II Corps coast; Phnom Penh and Sihanoukville (Kompong Som), Cambodia · new · EV003-2*
*Initiative: enemy*

Hanoi did not give up the sea at once. After TIDE TABLE's first half-year Group 125 paused through the summer of 1964, and in September, while the Global Hawk orbit was still held in the Gulf of Tonkin after the August crisis, it sent two trawlers south. One landed its cargo at Vung Ro on the night of 21–22 September in a gap the Gulf watch had opened on the II Corps coast; the other was tracked off Binh Dinh after the orbit returned, and was intercepted on 9 November by the Vietnamese Navy escort *Chi Lang* and a Junk Force division off the Tam Quan coast, where her crew beached and burned her. Sixteen of her crew were killed or drowned and four captured; the junk sailors lost two killed. Those are counts, and reliable. In December a third trawler reversed course off Quang Ngai at dusk when a Seventh Fleet destroyer closed her, and went home¹⁹.

The decision to change routes was taken in Hanoi in December 1964 and January 1965. Party records captured later in this war describe the Central Military Commission's conclusion that "the enemy now sees every ship at sea by day and by night" and that the route was no longer worth the ships and the men. The alternative was already prepared. Sihanouk, who had renounced American aid in November 1963 and was moving toward China, would break diplomatic relations with Washington on 3 May 1965. Through the winter Chinese and North Vietnamese negotiators in Phnom Penh arranged the passage of "rice and commercial goods" through Sihanoukville, the port the French had built at Kompong Som, with a commission to Cambodian officers at each stage, and Cambodian trucking contractors to carry the cargo inland along Route 4 to Kompong Speu and thence to the border base areas. Historically this arrangement took shape in late 1966. Here the first arms cargo for the Front came ashore at Sihanoukville under a Chinese flag in late June 1965, about eighteen months early, and was trucked to the depots behind the Fishhook and the Parrot's Beak by night¹⁹.

Two things followed from it that did not happen historically. The trawler that an Army helicopter pilot, Lieutenant James Bowers, found by chance unloading at Vung Ro Bay on 16 February 1965, with about 100 tons of arms aboard, was not there to be found; there was no trawler on the II Corps coast that month. And Market Time, which the Navy opened on 11 March 1965 historically because of Vung Ro, opened on the same date here because of TIDE TABLE's record, with a picture of what it was looking for and, increasingly, nothing to find¹⁹. The weapons the sea route would have landed in 1965 still reached the Front, more slowly and by a longer road, and from mid-1965 the enemy's principal line of supply to the southern half of the country ran through a neutral port that no American could stop or seize.

#### Operation LONG SPAN: the floods of November 1964
*6 Nov 1964 – 15 Feb 1965 · Route 1 and the coastal railway from Da Nang to Quang Ngai; Quang Nam, Quang Tin and Quang Ngai Provinces · new · EV008-2*
*Initiative: neither*

In the first week of November 1964 two typhoons and the rains behind them put the coastal plain of Quang Nam, Quang Tin and Quang Ngai under water, in the worst flood in central Vietnam in living memory; the dead were counted in thousands, by some Vietnamese accounts about 7,000, and Route 1 and the coastal railway were cut at dozens of points between the Hai Van pass and Quang Ngai. The ARVN 2nd Division in Quang Ngai and the province garrisons of the coast were cut off from Da Nang by road, and so were the rice and the relief.

In this war the bridges came back from Bien Hoa, in an operation the Directorate called LONG SPAN. The fabrication shops held a standard 30-metre steel truss span and a 12-metre girder span, drawn in 1963 for the outpost and road programme, and the Directorate had about forty of them in stock when the water fell. Between 14 and 30 November the shops cut and drilled about ninety more, and two Vietnamese Navy LSTs and three chartered coasters carried them from the Thu Duc yard to Da Nang and to the beach at Sa Huynh, where the 2nd Division's engineers and two Vietnamese contractor crews met them. Route 1 was open to the 2nd Division's trucks from Da Nang to Quang Ngai on 21 December, about six weeks after the flood and, by the Directorate's own estimate, two months sooner than spans ordered from Okinawa would have reopened it¹⁴. The railway took longer, as it would have in any case, and its last bridge south of Tam Ky was not back until 15 February.

The ninety-day road became the Directorate's term for its standard: any bridge the Front or the weather took on a national route was to be back in ninety days, and on Route 1 it usually was. It changed no battle in Phase I. Ba Gia was lost in May on ground the road did not decide. What it changed was the winter of 1964–65 on the coast: the rice of the Quang Ngai lowlands went north by truck in January instead of rotting in the district towns, the relief reached the flooded districts of Dien Ban and Duy Xuyen in December instead of February, and the Front's Region 5 committee, which had planned its winter around a coast cut off until the dry season, found the 2nd Division supplied by road¹⁴. On the historical course the flood was one of the reasons the coastal Centre was so nearly lost in the spring of 1965. Here it was a smaller one.

#### ◆ The night under the cloud at Hoai Nhon
*14–15 Dec 1964 · The PF post at Tam Quan and the district town of Hoai Nhon, Binh Dinh Province; Qui Nhon airfield · new · EV015-2*
*Initiative: enemy*

The night that ended the gunship's work below the cloud came in the first north-east monsoon of the war. At 2310 on 14 December the post at Tam Quan, on Route 1 north of Bong Son, called Hoai Nhon district that it was under attack by a company of the 2nd Regiment of the Front's Military Region 5. The cloud base over the coast was about 1,800 feet, with rain. Ghostrider 63 of the 14th Squadron, on station south of Qui Nhon, came north and found nothing on its turrets but the glow of the post's flares through the cloud. Its commander, Major Thomas R. Gaither, took the aircraft down through the overcast to 1,500 feet to see, as two crews of the squadron had done successfully in November²².

He found the attack at once and it found him. The company had two 12.7mm machine guns sited on the paddy dikes south of the post, and they opened on the aircraft's first turn. The Ghostrider fired for about six minutes and broke the assault on the southern wall; in those minutes it took about thirty rounds of 12.7mm through the left wing and the fuel tanks, lost an engine and a hydraulic system, and was flown back to Qui Nhon on three engines with a fire in the wing that the fuel-tank protection kept from spreading. Two crewmen were wounded. The post at Tam Quan held until 0400, when the attack was renewed in the rain with no aircraft overhead, and fell; nineteen of its thirty men were killed or taken²².

On 16 December the wing forbade attacks below 3,500 feet. From then on, on monsoon nights over the coast, the Ghostrider could give the posts of Binh Dinh, Quang Ngai and Quang Nam nothing but a GPS weapon on a coordinate the posts could not give it, and the Front's provincial units, which had believed since March that the aircraft saw through cloud, learned over the winter that it did not²². It was the provision's limit (D14), found in a night.

#### Saigon's governments and the war in the city
*19 Dec 1964 – 19 Jun 1965 · Saigon · changed from history · EVCP01-1*
*Initiative: both*

Six months of Saigon's politics are told here as one event, because they were one process: the generals unmaking every government they made, while the Front's city teams showed they could strike anywhere in the capital. In the week before Christmas 1964 the Young Turks, with Khanh's acquiescence, dissolved the High National Council and arrested several of its members; Taylor dressed the officers down, Khanh attacked him publicly, and the Mission's relations with the junta reached their lowest point³³. At 17:45 on 24 December a car bomb of about 90 kg under the Brink Bachelor Officers' Quarters killed two Americans and wounded about 60–100 people; Johnson refused a reprisal, saying he had "never felt that this war will be won from the air", and changed his mind within six weeks³⁷.

On 27 January 1965 Khanh and the Armed Forces Council removed Huong. On 16 February the council appointed Dr Phan Huy Quat prime minister, and on 19–20 February, in the confusion of a failed coup by General Lam Van Phat and Colonel Pham Ngoc Thao, who was a communist agent, it voted Khanh out; he left the country on 25 February³³. On 30 March a car carrying about 300 pounds of plastic explosive blew up outside the US embassy; 22 people were killed, among them two Americans, and 183 wounded. Rolling Thunder was already running, and there was no reprisal³⁸. On 12 June Quat, deadlocked with the chief of state Phan Khac Suu, handed power back to the generals; on 14 June Major General Nguyen Van Thieu became chief of state, and on 19 June Air Vice Marshal Nguyen Cao Ky prime minister³⁹.

In this war the sequence ran on its historical dates and for its historical reasons. One thing in it was different: each government that inherited the decrees of 1 January 1964 kept them, because Johnson's condition of February 1964 and the Aid Compliance Board had made repeal dearer than survival, and the September plotters' promise to repeal them had attached the reforms to the Young Turks who defeated them².

#### The Brink Hotel bombing and the purge of the High National Council
*19–24 Dec 1964 · Saigon · no longer happens · EV000-8*
*Consolidated at CP01 into EVCP01-1; the event still happens.*

#### The battle of Binh Gia
*28 Dec 1964 – 1 Jan 1965 · Binh Gia, Phuoc Tuy Province · changed from history · EV000-9*
*Initiative: enemy*

Binh Gia, a Catholic village of northern refugees in Phuoc Tuy Province east of Saigon, was the site of the Viet Cong's first large set-piece battle. The attackers were the 271st and 272nd Regiments (then called the 1st and 2nd), the nucleus of the forming 9th Division, with the 80th Artillery Detachment and local battalions of the B-2 Front, an estimated 1,800 men. They took the village on 28 December and then waited for the relief. The ARVN committed the 30th, 33rd, 35th and 38th Ranger Battalions and the 4th Marine Battalion of the general reserve, with two artillery platoons and a section of M24 tanks, about 4,300 men, lifted and supported by the US 118th Aviation Company and the gunships of the 68th Assault Helicopter Company⁴⁰. Every one of those battalions had carried the M4A1, the M249 and the M110A2 since the spring; the attackers carried Type 56 and SKS rifles, RPD guns and recoilless rifles²¹.

The relieving battalions were fed in piecemeal, the artillery badly positioned and air support thin, as they were historically. The rifles did not change the generals' plan, and the plan was the problem. On 30 December a 68th Company gunship was shot down near Quang Giao and its four crewmen killed. On 31 December the 4th Marine Battalion, going to recover the bodies, was ambushed in the rubber by the 271st at a range of 20 to 40 metres, as it was historically, and its commander, Major Nguyen Van Nho, was killed in the first minutes. What followed was different. The battalion's squad guns, three to a company platoon, put a base of fire into the trees that the Marines' BARs could not have sustained, and the companies, instead of being cut apart in the order they had marched, closed into two perimeters within about a quarter of an hour and fought out of the rubber at dusk carrying most of their wounded. The battalion lost about 60–70 killed and 90 wounded, against 122 killed and 71 wounded on the historical course²¹. Captain Donald Cook, the Marine adviser, was wounded and captured going back for a wounded Marine, as he was historically, and died in captivity. MACV counted allied losses from 28 December to 6 January at about 140 killed, 220 wounded and 45 missing⁴¹. MACV reported four helicopters shot down⁴⁰. The VC claim of 1,783 killed and captured is no more credible here than it was historically; their own dead, of whom at least 32 were confirmed historically, I put at about 120–200, because the Marines' marksmen and squad guns killed men in the rubber whom the 271st could not carry away⁴².

The significance was still the form. A Viet Cong formation had held ground for four days against armour, artillery and helicopters, chosen the field, ambushed the general reserve on the road it had to use, and withdrawn intact. The 271st took the honorific "Binh Gia Regiment"⁴⁰. US observers concluded, as they did historically, that the ARVN could not defeat main-force units of this kind without American firepower; what they could not say, as they said historically, was that a Marine battalion of the general reserve had been destroyed. The 4th Marines were back in the line in February.

The sergeants did the rest. The 4th Marine Battalion's NCOs had all passed the courses for their grades at Nha Trang since 1957, and its platoon sergeants the senior course; when Major Nho fell, two of the four company commanders were hit in the same minutes, and the companies were taken over by their first sergeants, who had been taught in the leader's course to count their men, establish a base of fire and report before they moved. Master Sergeant Nguyen Van Thuoc of the 2nd Company put his three squad guns on the trees and moved his platoons back by bounds; the companies closed into their two perimeters about five minutes faster than they would have done, and the second perimeter held the 271st's assault at 1640 without falling back into the first. I put the battalion's loss at about 50–60 killed and 100 wounded, against the 60–70 killed and 90 wounded of the earlier course, and MACV's allied losses for the battle at about 130 killed rather than about 140; the difference is wounded men who came out because a sergeant counted them⁶. The battle's form, which the 271st chose, did not change.

#### The red cards: Seoul, Canberra and the combined command
*8 Jan – 30 Jul 1965 · Seoul; Canberra; Saigon; Bien Hoa air base; Di An, Bien Hoa Province · new · EV012-3*
*Initiative: allied*

The Korean and Australian governments' consent to combined command, stipulated by this headquarters' instructions, was tested when their troops arrived. In Seoul the National Assembly debated the dispatch of the Dove Force of engineers and a security battalion in January 1965. The opposition asked whether Korean soldiers would be commanded by Americans abroad as they had refused to be commanded at home in matters of domestic order. President Park's defence minister, Kim Sung-eun, answered that they would be under the combined commander's operational control "for the tasks agreed between the governments and no others"¹⁶. That was the red card, stated in public. The Dove Force reached Di An in March under a Korean brigadier, and it took combined orders for its construction tasks without dispute, because it had no others to take¹⁷.

The Australians had already given their consent at a price. When 1st Battalion, Royal Australian Regiment arrived at Bien Hoa in June 1965 and joined the 173rd Airborne Brigade, Canberra's instructions to Brigadier O. D. Jackson, commanding Australian Army Force Vietnam, set three conditions on the combined command. The battalion would not be broken up below company level. It would not be employed in the Delta. And any operation expected to cost it more than a stated number of casualties would be referred to Canberra. The third condition was the one the combined staff had not expected. It meant that a combined order to the Australian battalion carried a casualty estimate, and that the estimate went to a capital. On 28 June, when the 173rd went into War Zone D, the combined staff's estimate for the battalion's part was sent to Canberra overnight and approved by 0600¹⁶. Nothing in the operation required the delay. The precedent did.

The Koreans' combat consent was the larger question, and it was settled on paper in July. The working arrangement that historically gave the Koreans MACV's support without American command (6 September 1965) was replaced here by a combined agreement of 23 July¹⁷. It placed the Korean field forces under the combined commander's operational control within an agreed area of responsibility on the II Corps coast, gave the Korean commander a right to refer any order outside that area to Seoul, and required a Korean officer to countersign any combined order to a Korean formation. Lieutenant General Chae Myung-shin signed it in Saigon. He told the combined commander, General William C. Westmoreland, that he would carry out every order he countersigned and countersign only the ones he would carry out¹⁶.

That sentence defined the Korean contingent for the rest of the war. It was operational control in name and a Korean veto in practice. The combined staff learned to write orders for the Korean divisions that the Korean commander had already agreed to. It was, I judge, a better arrangement than the historical one for exactly one reason: the Koreans' orders and the ARVN's now came from the same plan, so that the seam between the Korean area and the ARVN 22nd Division's was planned by one staff instead of negotiated by two¹⁶.

#### The fall of Khanh
*27 Jan – 25 Feb 1965 · Saigon · no longer happens · EV000-10*
*Consolidated at CP01 into EVCP01-1; the event still happens.*

#### The American escalation: Flaming Dart, the opening strike, the Marines and the July decision
*7 Feb – 28 Jul 1965 · Pleiku and Qui Nhon; North Vietnam; Da Nang; Washington · changed from history · EVCP01-2*
*Initiative: both*

The six months in which the United States went from an advisory war to a war of its own are told here as one event, because each step made the next one. At 01:50 on 7 February 1965, while McGeorge Bundy was in Saigon and Kosygin in Hanoi, about 300 men of the 30th Company of the VC 409th Sapper Battalion attacked Camp Holloway and the advisers' compound at Pleiku, killing seven Americans and wounding 104 and destroying 10 aircraft. About twelve hours later 49 aircraft from the *Coral Sea* and the *Hancock* struck the barracks at Dong Hoi, the first strike of Flaming Dart; after the VC blew up the enlisted billet at Qui Nhon on 10 February and killed 23 Americans, about 160 aircraft struck Chanh Hoa and Chap Le⁴³. Bundy's report of 7 February argued for "sustained reprisal", and the administration accepted it.

What the President approved on 13 February was not history's "measured and limited air action" south of the 19th parallel. In this war the theater's plan had been on his desk since the autumn: the Joint Chiefs' list of 94 targets of August 1964, extended by the MACV Combined Targeting Cell with the North's command centres, radars, power plants and bulk fuel, and a request that the campaign be run from Saigon, without zones around Hanoi and Haiphong and without pauses, with a buffer of 15 miles along the Chinese border and one strike on Haiphong's port. Johnson kept two things: the decision whether the bombing would happen at all, and its date. He gave the rest. Rolling Thunder opened on 2 March, history's date, with the opening strike of Operation HAMMER FALL on the whole list in place of history's 104 USAF aircraft at Xom Bang; the 19 VNAF A-1s still struck the naval base at Quang Khe that day, as they had historically⁴⁴. The route packages divided the North between the services from the spring, as historically, but the targets in every package came from Tan Son Nhut, not from the President's Tuesday lunch⁷.

On 8 March Battalion Landing Team 3/9 came ashore at Da Nang and BLT 1/3 flew in from Okinawa, the nearly 5,000 men of the 9th Marine Expeditionary Brigade under Brigadier General Frederick J. Karch, to guard the air base that Rolling Thunder needed and the Bien Hoa attack had shown to be vulnerable. Their orders, that they "will not, repeat will not, engage in day-to-day actions against the Viet Cong", lasted three weeks: on 1 April the President approved their use in active combat and 18,000–20,000 more support troops, and on 6 May the brigade became the III Marine Amphibious Force⁴⁵⁴⁶.

On 7 June Westmoreland asked for a major reinforcement and by 22 July the President had authorized 44 battalions⁴⁷. On 28 July, at a midday press conference rather than before Congress, Johnson announced that US strength would rise at once from 75,000 to 125,000, with more as requested, that monthly draft calls would rise from 17,000 to 35,000, and that the Airmobile Division would deploy; the reserves were not called and no new taxes were sought⁴⁸. Westmoreland's concept behind it, to halt the losing trend in 1965, take the offensive in 1966 and then destroy the enemy's main forces and base areas, committed the United States to attrition against an enemy who controlled his losses by deciding when to fight.

The ground war's steps came on their historical dates for their historical reasons. The air war over the North did not. Its opening came on history's day and its shape was the theater's, and by the July decision the North had already lost what history spent two and a half years taking from it. Nothing in that loss changed the decision of 28 July, which was driven by the ARVN's defeats of May and June at Ba Gia and Dong Xoai and not by anything in the North⁷.

#### Pleiku, Qui Nhon and Flaming Dart
*7–11 Feb 1965 · Camp Holloway and Pleiku, Pleiku Province; Qui Nhon, Binh Dinh Province; southern North Vietnam · no longer happens · EV000-11*
*Consolidated at CP01 into EVCP01-2; the event still happens.*

#### Operation NIGHT HERON: the Long An province committee
*9 Feb – 18 Apr 1965 · Can Giuoc, Can Duoc and Rach Kien Districts, Long An Province; the Rung Sat edge of Nha Be, Gia Dinh Province · new · EV014-2*
*Initiative: allied*

NIGHT HERON was the first operation that showed what the unit was for, and it was built on a file three other provisions had made. The task force's DRY SEASON campaign had broken the Can Giuoc security section from a notebook in December 1964; the section's papers named a courier, the courier's family was in the Special Branch's census, and the land register said whose house he slept in. What none of that could do was reach the eleven men of the Long An province committee, who met every third or fourth week at a sampan mooring on the Rach Cat where no police patrol had been since 1962.

Two men of G Squadron lay in the nipa palm 240 metres from the mooring for four nights between 9 and 23 February and for three more in March, counted the arrivals, and photographed six of them at ranges under 300 metres with a starlight-assisted camera. On the night of 14–15 March 1965 two assault teams and a Special Branch inspector came in by three of E Squadron's helicopters to a paddy 900 metres east, walked in on goggles, and took the mooring at 02:40. Six of the eleven committee members were there, with the province finance section's ledgers for 1964 and a list of the collectors in nineteen villages; two cadre who reached for weapons were killed, the committee's secretary got into the Rung Sat with the political officer, and one Delta operator was shot through the forearm. The task force's exploitation cell at Tan Son Nhut read the ledgers by 06:00 and the arrests ran for nine days: twenty-three collectors and two village committees taken on Special Branch warrants, with magistrate review in each case inside 72 hours under the 1964 statute³².

Long An's collections from the posted villages of the three districts fell by about two-thirds through the summer of 1965 and were about a third of the previous year's by December, which cost the Front both money and the rice it bought with it in a province where the titling had already ended rent³². The secretary rebuilt the committee from the Rung Sat by August with cadres sent down from the region, as the Can Giuoc section had been rebuilt before it. What did not come back was the mooring: the committee never again met in a place a boat could reach from a hamlet with a post.

#### Rolling Thunder begins
*2 Mar 1965 · North Vietnam, south of the 20th parallel · no longer happens · EV000-12*
*Consolidated at CP01 into EVCP01-2; the event still happens.*

#### Operation HAMMER FALL: the opening strike on the North
*2–9 Mar 1965 · Phuc Yen airfield, Hanoi (Bach Mai, the Citadel, Yen Phu, Duc Giang and the Paul Doumer bridge), Haiphong port and Thuong Ly, Nam Dinh, the Ham Rong bridge at Thanh Hoa, Vinh; Andersen Air Force Base, Guam; Yankee Station; Korat and Takhli, Thailand · new · EV013-1*
*Initiative: allied*

The plan began on 5 August 1964, the morning after the false alarm in the Gulf that this war never answered with Pierce Arrow. Major Edwin T. Sorrell's coordinate cell at Tan Son Nhut, which had measured the panhandle since March, was given the North above the 20th parallel, and Brigadier General Walter E. Pruitt of the 2nd Air Division was sent to build a MACV Combined Targeting Cell around it, with Captain Harlan J. Reese of CINCPAC's staff as his deputy for the Navy. The Joint Chiefs' study of 24 August gave them 94 targets in five categories, airfields, lines of communication, military installations, industry and the routes of armed reconnaissance. The cell added what the list held thinly: the Air Defence–Air Force Command and its operations centre at Bach Mai, the Ministry of Defence and General Staff in the Citadel, the national radio transmitters at Me Tri, the long-haul exchanges, the early-warning radars, the Yen Phu and Haiphong power plants and the transformer stations between them, and the bulk fuel at Duc Giang, Thuong Ly, Nam Dinh and Vinh. By February 1965 the U-2S had imaged every one of them under the standing condition's satellite link, and the cell held about 760 desired points of impact, each measured to a few metres⁷.

The President's order of 13 February set the day as 2 March. The crachin set the hour. On the night of 2–3 March the cloud over the Red River delta sat at about 800–1,500 feet with drizzle, and nothing but the bombers could strike (§1.1 D4 of memorandum 013). Twenty-four B-52Js of the 7th and 320th Bombardment Wings left Andersen in cells of three from 15:00 local time, flew about 2,200 nautical miles to entry points over the Gulf of Tonkin, and released from 36,000–40,000 feet between 23:40 and 01:15, from up to fifteen miles out. The order of the night was fixed by what could fight back. The first two cells put 70 GBU-38s on Phuc Yen's runway intersections and its 38 revetments, and destroyed or wrecked 22–26 of the 921st Fighter Regiment's 36 MiG-17Fs on the ground; eight that were on alert in the northern dispersal survived, and two of them flew to Kep at first light. The third cell struck the early-warning radars at Bach Mai, Phu Ly and Kep and the four fire-control radars the cell had located, all with GBU-38. Then the penetrators: twelve GBU-31(V)3/Bs on the Bach Mai operations centre, sixteen on the Ministry of Defence buildings in the Citadel, eight on the Me Tri transmitters. Then the fixed economy: the Yen Phu power station and Haiphong's, the transformer yards at Dong Anh and Thai Binh, the tank farms at Duc Giang and Thuong Ly, two spans of the Paul Doumer bridge over the Red River, the Ham Rong bridge at Thanh Hoa with twenty penetrators on its abutments and central pier, and the rail and road bridges at Nam Dinh, Ninh Binh and Phu Ly⁷.

The last target of the night was Haiphong's port, its one strike. At dusk the Gulf watch had counted fourteen ships in the port, among them three Soviet, two Polish and one British freighter alongside the main quay. Six Js put 120 GBU-31(V)1/Bs on the gantry and portal cranes, the transit sheds and warehouses behind the coal quay, the port's substation and the lighter basin, and none on the berths with ships alongside. The British *Dartford* lost her gangway and a lifeboat to blast; no seaman of any flag died. The port's cranes, about forty of them, were destroyed or toppled; the North's own figures, given to the Polish member of the International Control Commission, were 214 port workers killed⁷.

No J was lost or hit. The guns of the delta, about 1,500–2,100 of them, 37mm and 57mm, fired blind into cloud at aircraft five miles above their reach. The second wave flew on the first days the cloud lifted, 4 and 7 March: alpha strikes of 30–40 aircraft from the *Hancock* and the *Ranger* on Kep, Gia Lam and the guns around the struck bridges, and F-105D packages from Korat and Takhli with F-100D flak suppression on the Hanoi and Haiphong rail yards, Thai Nguyen's blast furnace under construction and the Viet Tri chemical works. The MiG-17s that had reached Kep rose on 7 March and lost two to F-8Es; the guns, now firing at aircraft under the cloud, shot down four F-105Ds, two A-4Cs and an F-8E in the week, with three pilots killed, two captured and two recovered at sea. Two more cells of Js restruck the Doumer bridge and the Haiphong power plant on 6 March on the U-2S's imagery of the 5th, the first orders of the restrike watch⁷.

By 9 March the North had lost, on this command's count checked against the U-2S, about 70–80 percent of its generating capacity, most of its bulk fuel in storage, the Doumer, Ham Rong and Nam Dinh bridges, its only jet base and most of its jet fighters, its warning net, and the cranes of its only deepwater port. Hanoi radio was off the air for nine hours and its air defence command was in the provincial switchboards for three weeks. No member of the Politburo or the Central Military Commission was killed or, as far as anyone in Saigon could tell, inconvenienced; the Ministry's buildings had been empty but for duty officers, and General Van Tien Dung's staff had worked from Hoa Lac since February. The North's civilian dead of the week, by Hanoi's count given in Geneva in April, were about 1,700; this command's own estimate was 800–1,500, most in Hanoi and Haiphong. The trucks of Group 559, the porters in the panhandle and the Front's regiments in the South noticed nothing at all⁷.

#### The restricted area over Trung Lap
*7 March 1965 · Trung Lap and Phu Hoa Dong, Hau Nghia Province · new · EV014-9*
*Initiative: allied*

The provision's first near-disaster was procedural and it is worth one event, because the procedure is most of what the new headquarters was for. On the night of 7 March 1965 an assault team raided a house at Trung Lap on a Hau Nghia file. MACV-JSOC's operations centre declared a restricted operating area of two kilometres' radius for four hours and passed it, as its standing procedure required, to the ARVN 25th Division, the province chief, the sector adviser and the combined command's III Corps centre³². It did not reach the Popular Forces platoon at the post 1,400 metres away, because the sector's night net closed at 22:00 and the platoon had no radio of its own until the Go Vap sets arrived later that year. The platoon heard helicopters and small-arms fire at 01:50 inside its own village, stood to, and its leader took a squad up the hamlet path toward the noise. Delta's team, coming out with a prisoner, identified the squad at about 120 metres through goggles and went flat in a ditch until it passed; nobody fired.

Vance changed the procedure within the week: from 15 March a restricted area was passed down to the platoon holding the ground or it was not declared, which in the first year cancelled eleven raids outright³². The incident is the clearest statement in the record of what the headquarters' deconfliction duty costs and buys. It buys the lives of the men on both ends of a hamlet path. It costs eleven targets in a year, and it is the reason a provision that needs a secret needs a joint headquarters more than it needs another squadron.

#### The Marines land at Da Nang
*8 Mar – 6 May 1965 · Da Nang, Quang Nam Province · no longer happens · EV000-13*
*Consolidated at CP01 into EVCP01-2; the event still happens.*

#### Operation STILL WATER
*8 Mar – 30 Jun 1965 · Hoc Mon and Go Vap Districts, Gia Dinh Province, inside the Hop Tac ring northwest of Saigon · new · EV006-2*
*Initiative: allied*

STILL WATER was the first operation in the war aimed at a district committee rather than at the hamlets beneath it, and it was the case for the method. By February 1965 Hoc Mon had 11 village posts settled under the police decree, a Popular Forces platoon in each, about 70 percent of its families on the census, and a district chief from the first list, Major Pham Van Truc; the Front's hamlet cells in the posted villages had mostly been arrested or had left. What remained was the Hoc Mon district committee of the Saigon–Gia Dinh Region, about 14 members with a security section, a finance section and about 40 couriers, which had moved in January into the hamlets along the Saigon River at the district's northern edge, toward the Ben Cat and Cu Chi base areas, and governed the posted villages from there by messenger. The DIOCC at Hoc Mon, opened in July 1964 under Captain Ngo Van Tam of the Special Branch with 12 staff and two American advisers, had by February a chart of that committee with 31 names, 19 of them confirmed on three sources, built from 44 arrests, 23 ralliers and a cache of finance-section receipts found by a PF patrol in December¹¹.

The operation was Tam's plan, approved by Truc and by the Gia Dinh province chief on 1 March. It used no regular troops. Two Police Field Force companies and the Gia Dinh PRU of about 80 men made the arrests; the Regional Forces companies of Hoc Mon and Go Vap, with M4A1 carbines and their M249s, held the cordons at night; a reaction platoon of the Gia Dinh sector and two UH-1Bs of the 120th Aviation Company stood by at Tan Son Nhut for the reaction; and every arrest went first to the biometric kit and then to a magistrate within 72 hours. Captain Whitcomb's counterpart in the next province was not involved; the American subsector adviser at Hoc Mon, Captain Luis Arredondo, was, and by the rule of the decree he did not go on the raids (EC006-13 abandoned)¹¹.

The turn came on the night of 26–27 March. A finance-section courier arrested on 19 March, a woman of 19 who carried tax receipts in the hem of a conical hat, rallied on the 24th after the Chieu Hoi officer offered her brother's release from a prison on Con Son, to which he had been sent in 1962 under Diem without trial, and named the house in Tan Thoi Nhi where the committee's security section met twice a month. The PRU went in at 0300 under a moon nearly full with two Police Field Force platoons in the cordon. Four men were taken alive in the house, including the section's deputy chief, with a steel box of informant reports on the posts of four villages; a fifth was killed in the paddy behind it after firing on the cordon with a K-50 sub-machine gun; one PRU member died. The box named nine of the committee's own informants inside the posted villages, three of them PF soldiers¹¹.

By 30 June the operation had arrested 63 people on warrant, of whom the magistrates released 17; 9 of the committee's 14 members were dead, in prison or had rallied, 2 of them killed resisting arrest; the remaining five had moved north into Cu Chi. The Commission's first audit sample, drawn in July from STILL WATER's files, found 4 of the 63 arrests unsupported and the release of one man already paid for, and sent both cases to the Special Court. The Front did not rebuild the Hoc Mon committee inside the district; it governed Hoc Mon from Cu Chi until 1968, and the tax receipts captured in 1966 showed collections in the district's posted villages at about a fifth of 1964's¹¹. In this war STILL WATER was a small operation, 63 arrests and three deaths in four months, and it changed the war in one particular: it showed the Front that the government could follow its leaders out of the villages it had cleared, three years before the historical Phoenix tried to.

#### Moscow, Beijing and the rebuilt sky
*10 Mar – 31 Jul 1965 · Moscow; Beijing; Hanoi; the frontier crossing at Pingxiang and Dong Dang; the airfields of Mengzi and Ningming in Yunnan and Guangxi · new · EV013-2*
*Initiative: enemy*

The answer to HAMMER FALL was decided in three capitals, and each decided for its own reasons. In Hanoi the Politburo under Le Duan, at its meeting of 25–27 March, ordered the North onto a war footing, the dispersal of every factory, depot and fuel store that could be moved, and a militia of the whole people for air defence and repair; and, reading the strike as the preparation for a landing, held the 308th and 312th Divisions and two regiments of the 320th in the delta and on the coast through the summer instead of preparing them for the South. In Moscow, Brezhnev and Kosygin, whose premier had left Hanoi on 10 February promising help, accelerated the air-defence package: SA-2 equipment for four battalions instead of two by July, about 1,000 advisers and technicians, MiG-21F-13s for the autumn, and diesel generators, pumps and trucks in place of the heavy plant the North had asked for in 1964. In Beijing, Mao and Zhou Enlai sent Chinese railway, engineer and anti-aircraft troops into the North from June, earlier and in larger numbers than they had historically, and placed three anti-aircraft divisions along the northeast and northwest railways, some inside the 15-mile buffer the Americans had left⁷.

Beijing's own decision, taken for its own reasons, slowed Moscow's. In April Moscow asked for an air corridor across China for Antonov transports carrying SA-2 equipment and its crews, and for the use of two airfields in Yunnan; the Chinese leadership refused both, as it did historically, and required every Soviet shipment to cross China by rail under Chinese escort and at Chinese pace. The refusal had nothing to do with the opening strike. It came from Beijing's quarrel with Moscow over the leadership of the communist world, and from Mao's suspicion that a Soviet presence in Yunnan was a Soviet presence on China's frontier. Its effect was that the first Soviet battalions of SA-2 reached Hanoi in late April and May by train through Pingxiang, three to five weeks later than Moscow had promised and with about a fifth of their equipment held at the border for inspection. The VPAF was given what Moscow could not use: its surviving MiG-17s and their pilots were moved to Mengzi and Ningming in China to train and re-form, and from the autumn its regiments flew into the North from Chinese fields to fight and back to rest⁷.

By the end of July the North had about 2,500–3,000 guns rather than history's rising count, four SA-2 battalions in the Hanoi area rotating among about twenty prepared sites, and a fighter force that lived in China. Its fuel was in drums under the trees, its power in about 700 village diesels from the Soviet shipments, and its bridges were pontoons by night. It had begun to pay for the war in labour: perhaps 300,000–400,000 people were repairing, carrying and manning guns full time by July, a year before history's figure reached that size. Its rice imports from China were promised for the autumn, about 200,000 tons for 1966⁷.

#### The bombing of the US embassy
*30 Mar 1965 · Saigon · no longer happens · EV000-14*
*Consolidated at CP01 into EVCP01-1; the event still happens.*

#### The air war of 1965: the Thanh Hoa Bridge, the missiles and the first Arc Light
*3 Apr – 27 Jul 1965 · Thanh Hoa (Ham Rong) and the Hanoi region, North Vietnam; Andersen Air Force Base, Guam; Ben Cat, Binh Duong Province · changed from history · EVCP02-2*
*Initiative: allied*

The four months after HAMMER FALL were the months in which the North rebuilt and the cell learned how fast. The Ham Rong bridge over the Song Ma at Thanh Hoa, which history's 79-aircraft package under Lieutenant Colonel Robinson Risner hit repeatedly and failed to drop on 3 April, had been in the river since the first night of March. On 3 April Risner's wing flew instead against the ferries and the pontoon bridge the North had built a kilometre upstream, and against the guns that now ringed it; the MiG-17s of the 921st Fighter Regiment that history sent against the F-105s on 4 April had been destroyed at Phuc Yen or were in China, and Major Frank Bennett and Captain J. A. Magnusson came home that week⁴⁹. The folder for Ham Rong, complete since the previous August, became the model for every bridge in the North: the U-2S's dawn pass found each pontoon, and a cell of Js or a package of F-105Ds struck it within a day or two. The requests of April and June for Js against the Dragon's Jaw, refused historically and in the record, were never needed⁷.

The missiles were seen coming and struck as they came. The U-2S's change detection showed the six-pointed pattern of an SA-2 site southeast of Hanoi on 22 March, its signals payload heard the first Fan Song emissions on 18 April, and by 24 July it had mapped about twenty prepared sites¹⁹. Under the provision there was no Soviet-technician rule: each site was struck by a cell of Js with GBU-38s on its revetments and van pads within 24–48 hours of its first image, and from May each occupied site the signals payload located was struck the same night. The North answered by preparing three or four sites for every battalion and moving the battalions by night, which was history's answer brought forward a year. The first SA-2 to bring down an American aircraft in this war was fired on 22 August, not 24 July, and Captain Roscoe Fobair's F-4C of the 47th Tactical Fighter Squadron was not lost; Spring High was never flown, because there was nothing in July for it to answer⁴⁴. Over the five months to the end of July about 60–75 American aircraft were lost over the North, against perhaps 85–100 in history's same months⁵⁰, nearly all of them to guns at low level over the restruck bridges and yards⁷.

The first Arc Light, on 18 June, was flown by thirty B-52Js of the 7th and 320th Bombardment Wings from Andersen in place of the historical B-52Fs, to the South and back without tankers, so the collision over the sea that killed eight crewmen historically did not happen. The Soviet trawler off Guam still gave Hanoi about three hours' notice; the tanker track that had betrayed the bombers' heading was gone. Twenty-eight aircraft struck a suspected base area near Ben Cat, about 40 miles north of Saigon, with about 51 bombs each⁵¹; two carried twenty GBU-31s each onto four structures the U-2S had imaged, and found four huts empty for days. The rest found what history found, little, and the press called it a sledgehammer swung at flies, now accurate to a few metres. It was the find-and-fix problem in its purest form, and the J changed none of it. Arc Light would run to August 1973 and fly about 126,615 sorties⁵²; here with 10–20 percent fewer maintenance men, no tanker bridge, and a carpet half as dense when it carried unguided bombs²⁵²⁴. By the end of July the B-52's war had two halves: over the North a bomber that had destroyed in a week what history's fighter-bombers spent years on, and over the South a bomber that found nothing under the canopy⁷.

#### The Thanh Hoa Bridge and the northern air defences
*3 Apr – 27 Jul 1965 · Thanh Hoa (Ham Rong), Thanh Hoa Province, and the Hanoi region · no longer happens · EV000-15*
*Consolidated at CP02 into EVCP02-2; the event still happens.*

#### The Vinh Long suspension
*6 Apr – 11 May 1965 · Vinh Long Province · new · EV001-5*
*Initiative: allied*

At the December 1964 harvest landlords' agents collected rent in four villages of Vinh Long's Tam Binh and Vung Liem districts under the escort of Regional Forces platoons the province chief had lent them, and tenants who refused were taken to the district headquarters. The Commission documented 26 cases from the tenants' complaints to the land court on circuit and from the American province adviser's reports, and on 2 February 1965 sent them to the Ministry of the Interior with 30 days to act. Nothing was done; Saigon was occupied with the fall of Khanh. On 6 April the Aid Compliance Board suspended the release of counterpart piasters to Vinh Long Province. On 28 April the Quat government replaced the province chief, and on 11 May, after the Commission certified that the escorts had ended and the collected rents had been returned, the Board restored the funds².

It was the only suspension the Board made against a province in the war's first two years, and it worked as designed: the next province chief stopped the escorts, and in the rest of the Delta the threat was enough. It also cost what this headquarters had warned it would cost. Liberation Radio broadcast for a month that the "American masters" had dismissed a "puppet" who displeased them, and in Saigon the officers who resented the Board pointed to the same fact².

#### The battle of Song Be
*10–12 May 1965 · Song Be (Phuoc Long province capital) and Phuoc Binh, Phuoc Long Province · no longer happens · EV000-16*
*Consolidated at CP01 into EVCP01-3; the event still happens.*

#### The battle of Ba Gia
*28–31 May 1965 · Ba Gia, Son Tinh District, Quang Ngai Province · changed from history · EV000-17*
*Initiative: enemy*

On 29 May 1965 the VC 1st Regiment of the forming 2nd Division (40th, 60th and 90th Battalions, with the 45th Independent Battalion and local force battalions), directed by PAVN Major General Chu Huy Man, overran the outpost at Loc Tho near Ba Gia, about 10 km from Quang Ngai, and ambushed the ARVN 1st Battalion, 51st Infantry Regiment as it came to help. On the historical course the battalion was destroyed. On this one it was cut in two on the road from Ba Gia and lost its headquarters company and most of one rifle company in the first half hour; the other three companies, each with a squad gun in every squad and a marksman in most, formed a perimeter on the low ridge north of the road and held it until dark, and about 280 of the battalion's men came back to Quang Ngai over the next two days. Major General Nguyen Chanh Thi, commanding I Corps, sent a task force under the 51st Regiment: its 2nd Battalion, the 3rd Marine Battalion, the 37th and 39th Ranger Battalions, two 105mm artillery battalions and an M113 squadron. On 30 May the 39th Rangers fought the VC 45th Battalion on Mount Chop Non, and the 2nd Battalion, 51st and the Marines were hit near Phuoc Loc; US fighter-bombers and UH-1 gunships, delayed because forward air controllers could not find the fight, prevented a rout⁴⁷. On the night of 30–31 May the 3rd Marines held a moat line through repeated assaults, with nine M249s across the line firing under illumination from the 105s, and their marksmen picking off the 40th Battalion's machine-gun teams setting up in the paddy dikes beyond the moat at 300–400 metres. The 39th Rangers were reduced to about two companies, against one historically²¹. The VC withdrew at dawn on 31 May.

I put ARVN losses at about 75 killed, 110 wounded and 190 captured or missing, against the US Army's historical figures of 107 killed, 123 wounded and 367 captured or missing; the individual weapons lost, 384 historically, I put at about 230, most of them M4A1 carbines with their sights⁵³.
