*4 Mar – 31 Oct 1967 · Duc Hoa, Hau Nghia Province, and the 25th Division's districts in Hau Nghia and Long An; the Joint General Staff and the Officer Selection Board, Saigon · new · EV007-4*
*Initiative: allied*

The 25th Division was the ARVN's worst, and in 1967 it became the test of whether the ranked list could reach a division. Its battalions, in Hau Nghia and western Long An between Saigon and the Parrot's Beak, had the highest desertion rate and the lowest rate of contact in III Corps, its regiments had been commanded by the division commander's appointees until the board's lists of 1965–66, and its commander, appointed in 1965 before the list existed, had refused to move his headquarters out of Duc Hoa at night. When he was due for reassignment in March 1967 the board's ranked list put three colonels before the Directory. Ky wanted a fourth man, an Air Force patron's cousin who was not on it; Thieu, as Chief of State, took the second name, Colonel Vu Ngoc Hoan, a Dalat graduate of 1952 who had commanded a regiment of the 7th Division well under the board's list, after the US Mission told both men through the Aid Compliance Board that the division's counterpart funds depended on the list (Article 14). It was one of two division commands in ten filled from the list against a palace preference by the end of 1967¹².

Hoan relieved three battalion commanders in his first ninety days, all through the board's thirty-day reviews and all for what the reviews called failure to close with the enemy, and put the division's battalions into the districts at night beside the Popular Forces, which the corps had never asked it to do. By October the division's desertion rate had fallen by about a quarter, its battalions were in contact about twice as often as in 1966, and the Front's district companies in Duc Hoa and Duc Hue had moved their bases toward the Cambodian border¹². None of this changed who held most of Hau Nghia's hamlets after dark, which the division could visit and could not hold.

In July the board extended its lists to the commanders of the Regional Force companies of III and IV Corps, at the request of the III Corps commander after the losses of the RF convoys on Route 13. About 280 RF company commanders were reviewed by the end of the year and about 60 replaced, most of them by field-commissioned lieutenants of their own provinces¹².

On 11 October the Military Security Service arrested Lieutenant Truong Cong Hy, a clerk of the board's secretariat since 1965, as an agent of the B2 Front's military intelligence. He had copied every command list the board had published, with the panels' ratings of each selected officer, and passed them by courier to the Cu Chi district committee. The COSVN target notes taken with his courier in November showed what the lists had been used for: the B2 Front's regiments had been told which battalions in III Corps were under new commanders and which were not, and had planned their ambushes of 1967 against the second¹². The board's best evidence of its own effect came from the man who had betrayed it.

#### The Special Court convicts the IV Corps quartermaster
*6 Mar – 18 Apr 1967 · Can Tho, Phong Dinh Province, and the Special Court for Corruption, Saigon · new · EV001-7*
On 6 March 1967 the Commission Against Corruption arrested Brigadier General Phan Duy Khiem, IV Corps deputy commander for logistics since 1965, with eleven officers of the corps quartermaster and finance staffs at Can Tho. The case was documentary and had been built for fourteen months. The Pay and Muster Verification Service's parade payments had removed ghost pay from the corps' units in 1964–65, and the graft had moved, as this headquarters had expected, to rations and fuel. The Service's auditors matched the corps' monthly ration draws against the strength the musters had established and found the corps drawing rice, canned fish and salt for about 9,000 more men than it had; a parallel audit by the Joint Aid Compliance Board traced about 2.1 million litres of motor fuel issued to RF companies with no vehicles and sold through two Cholon dealers. The total over two years came to about 310 million piasters, about $2.6 million at the official rate².

The arrest was made by the Commission's own judicial police, Vietnamese officers under Commissioner Khoa, with no American present. General Khiem was the first general officer the Commission had charged, three years after it was founded; it had refused the junta's invitations to begin at the top in 1964 and had worked upward from battalion and district. His patron in the Directory tried to have the case moved to a military court in March; the Deputy Chief of Mission, as American co-chair of the Board, told the Directory's chairman, General Thieu, that the US Mission would suspend counterpart funds to IV Corps under the Board's authority if the case was moved, and Thieu, then preparing his presidential candidacy and wanting no quarrel with the Embassy, let it stand. The Special Court tried the case in public in Saigon over nine sitting days and on 18 April 1967 sentenced Khiem to eight years and confiscation of property, and nine of the eleven officers to terms of two to six years².

The conviction mattered more in the officer corps than in the countryside. It established that the Commission's reach now ran to the rank below the Directory and no higher; that it reached that rank only on documentary evidence and with the American lever behind it; and that a general who stole from his troops' rations could go to prison. The American press reported it as a sign that the Saigon government was cleaning itself up, and for a few weeks the corruption stories of the spring were about a conviction rather than a scandal. The officers of the IV Corps staff who replaced the convicted were chosen in part from the Commission's own list of officers with clean records, and there were not enough of them².

#### ◆ The night of the relief at My Phuoc Tay
*9–10 Mar 1967 · The PF post at Ap My Loi and the canal road to Cai Lay district town, My Phuoc Tay village, Cai Lay District, Dinh Tuong Province · new · EV008-4*
*Initiative: enemy*

This is the night the radio's second-order effect became visible, and the night that shows it best. By 1967 every Popular Forces post in the held belt of Dinh Tuong had a set on the district net, a PRC-25 from the Go Vap plant since the previous autumn, and a fresh battery every Thursday from the exchange at the district¹⁴. The Front had stopped assaulting those posts in 1965, as it had stopped assaulting the rearmed ones. In the first months of 1967 the Dinh Tuong provincial unit decided to use the radio against the men it called.

At 2235 on 9 March a mortar section of the 514th Local Force Battalion put twelve 60mm rounds into the post at Ap My Loi, a mud-walled fort on the canal six kilometres north of Cai Lay, from about 300 metres across the paddy. Sergeant Le Van Muoi, who commanded its PF platoon, called the district at 2237, reported the mortar and an assault forming on the dike to the west, and asked for the reaction force. The assault did not come. Two companies of the 514th were not on the dike at all; they were lying in the sugar cane on both sides of the canal road, three kilometres south of the post, where the road crossed a concrete culvert that the district's trucks had to slow to cross¹⁴.

The district chief, Major Nguyen Thanh Liem, did what the rule of 1964 told him, that a post that called would have its reaction within the hour, and sent the 247th Regional Force Company north in four trucks at 2250. At 2304 a command-detonated mine under the culvert lifted the lead truck off the road. The firing circuit, the district's engineers found in the morning, was two kilometres of WD-1 field wire from the Republic's own mill and a pack of Go Vap dry cells of the kind the post had been issued the week before. The two companies fired into the other three trucks from both sides for about eight minutes, took the weapons from the first, and were gone into the cane before the 7th Division's battery at Cai Lay had been given a target. The 247th lost 31 dead and 18 wounded and about 40 weapons, most of them M4A1 carbines and two M249s. The post itself lost one man, wounded by the mortar. The district reported 46 of the enemy killed; eight bodies were found at the culvert in the morning, and I believe the 514th lost between ten and fifteen killed¹⁴.

In this war there was a gunship over Dinh Tuong that night, and the Front had planned for it. At 2220, fifteen minutes before the mortar opened on Ap My Loi, a company of the 261st Main Force Battalion attacked the post at Long Dinh in Chau Thanh district, 25 kilometres east, with mortars and a ground probe, and the Ghostrider of the 4th Squadron on station over the province went there, as the post's call and the district's relay told it to. It was firing on the 261st's company in the rice east of Long Dinh when the mine went off under the culvert at Cai Lay, and by the time it reached the canal road at 2318 the two companies of the 514th were in the cane, moving north-west by squads, and the crew found and fired on only one of them as it crossed the open paddy toward the Plain of Reeds²². The 514th's dead at the culvert and in the paddy I now put at about 20–30. The 261st's attack at Long Dinh was a diversion, the first the Front had made to draw the gunship off; it was not the last.

Liem wrote new rules the following week, and they are the reason the night belongs in the history. A post's first call would be for fire, not men: every post in the district had its concentrations plotted for the 105mm battery at Cai Lay and the 7th Division's guns at Cai Be, and the call was answered by the guns within four minutes. A relief would move by water or at first light, never by road at night on the one road the Front could predict. And a post that could hold behind its wire would be expected to. The rules spread through IV Corps in the spring, carried by the province advisers and by the 7th Division's artillery officers. In the months that followed, attacks on posts within range of the guns in Dinh Tuong fell by about a third, and the Front's district companies turned to the posts beyond the guns' 11 kilometres and to the posts whose batteries were run down at the end of the exchange week¹⁴.

The rules had a cost that the returns did not show and Liem's adviser did. A platoon that could call down the guns had less reason to leave its wire after dark, and in 1967 the night patrols of the PF platoons in Cai Lay fell by about a third; the post held itself, and the hamlet outside it was walked at night, more often than before, by the Front's tax collectors¹⁴. Ap My Loi held every attack made on it for the rest of the war. Whether the hamlet that bore its name was the government's after dark is a question the post's radio log cannot answer.

Sergeant Muoi had passed the leader's course at Nha Trang in 1961 and the platoon leader's course at the Dinh Tuong centre in 1964, and he kept a listening post of two men on the canal bank 300 metres south of the post every night. At 2241 they reported men moving south in the cane on the far side of the canal, toward the road, and Muoi added it to his report: the mortar, an assault forming to the west, and movement south toward the culvert. The district operations room logged it as the third of three lines. Liem read the first two and sent the trucks, because the rule said the reaction within the hour and the post had called; the sergeant had seen the ambush and the decision was not his⁶. The ambush went as told. The third of Liem's new rules came from Muoi's line in the log: a post's report of movement on the relief route stops the relief until the route is fired on or cleared at first light, and the district's posts were taught, through their platoon leaders, to report the road as well as the wire.

Liem's fourth rule came from the night's other lesson. No relief would move by road at night unless a gunship flew the road ahead of it, and the province's two gunship sorties a night were assigned in pairs from April, one over the posts and one held for the road. On the night of 14 June 1967 the 247th RF Company moved north to Ap My Loi again, under a post's call, with a Ghostrider over the canal road two kilometres ahead of the trucks; its turret found about fifty men lying in the cane at the culvert, the trucks stopped, and the 105mm and 30mm fired on the cane for twenty minutes. The 514th left 26 dead and two 57mm recoilless rifles at the culvert, and the 247th reached the post at 0130 without a man hit²². It did not lay an ambush on that road again on a clear night.

#### The Hill Fights at Khe Sanh
*24 Apr – 11 May 1967 · Hills 861, 881 South and 881 North, northwest of Khe Sanh, Quang Tri Province · no longer happens · EV000-34*
*Consolidated at CP02 into EVCP02-3; the event still happens.*

#### CORDS is established
*9 May 1967 · Saigon · changed from history · EV000-35*
*Initiative: allied*
On 9 May 1967 the President placed the American pacification effort, civil and military, under MACV as Civil Operations and Revolutionary Development Support, with Robert W. Komer as one of Westmoreland's deputies, holding ambassadorial rank and authority over military personnel and resources, the first civilian to serve in such a role inside a US military command. It followed the failure of the Office of Civil Operations, set up in November 1966 to coordinate the civil agencies under civilian leadership. CORDS was about 85 percent military. Each corps had a CORDS deputy, usually civilian, under the corps senior adviser, and each province an American province senior adviser with civilian and military staffs alongside the Vietnamese province chief, usually an ARVN colonel⁷⁹.

Komer's priorities were the territorial forces and the attack on the VC infrastructure. The Regional and Popular Forces numbered about 300,000 in 1967; their advisory presence was later multiplied through Mobile Advisory Teams⁷⁹. In mid-1967 CORDS created the Intelligence Coordination and Exploitation programme, ICEX, to target the VCI, which became the Phoenix programme at the end of the year. The Hamlet Evaluation System, introduced in January 1967, began rating every hamlet monthly on a scale reported by the American district advisers; its figures were at once an indispensable record and a measure produced by officers rated on the result. CORDS solved the American management problem of pacification. It did not solve the Vietnamese one, that security in the hamlet at night depended on Vietnamese forces and officials who had to stay there.

In this war CORDS is established on the historical date and in the historical form; the provisions did not create a single manager. What CORDS inherits is different. The American land advisers, about 260 in 1964 and about 340 by 1967, pass into it as a land reform division under the CORDS deputy, and the provincial land services' register of titled parcels, which by May 1967 lists about 740,000 hectares and the households that hold them, becomes the first hamlet-by-hamlet household record the American pacification effort has ever had⁸. Komer adds titled hectares as a factor in the Hamlet Evaluation System's economic rating from its first revision. This headquarters objected in writing on 22 June 1967 that a title measured the government's paperwork and not the peasant's allegiance, that the Front had told its cadres to let peasants take titles and keep paying the Front's tax, and that titles issued would rise fastest where they meant least. The objection was recorded and the factor kept, at half the weight Komer had proposed. The Phung Hoang centres, which had used the land register since 1964 to locate the families of known cadres, passed into CORDS's reporting with it; the Commission had been receiving complaints about that use since 1965²¹¹.

In this war CORDS inherits a police as well as a land register. The aid mission's Public Safety Division, about 300 advisers in 1966, passes into CORDS as its Public Safety Directorate, and with it a National Police of about 70,000, about 1,500 village posts, and a Special Branch with district sections in about 200 districts and files on the infrastructure kept since 1964⁹. There is no ICEX in this war, because there is nothing for it to create: the Phung Hoang centres have sat in those district sections since 1964, under the district chief, with the Military Security Service and the sector S2 in the room, and every district has one by the end of 1965. The Provincial Reconnaissance Units were raised under the police statute in 1964 and answer to the province chiefs, not to the Agency; their methods are inside the statute's safeguards in law, and Duc Thanh in February showed how far that law reached in a village the government did not hold at night. Komer's contribution was the money to put a centre's American adviser in every contested district, which Hieu accepted, and a proposal for monthly targets, which the decree forbade¹¹. What the American pacification effort created in 1967 on the historical course, here it found already running, and the province senior advisers found a counterpart chief who had been in his post for two or three years³.

#### Operation Buffalo and the siege of Con Thien
*2 Jul – 31 Oct 1967 · Con Thien and "the Marketplace", Quang Tri Province, south of the DMZ · no longer happens · EV000-36*
*Consolidated at CP02 into EVCP02-3; the event still happens.*

#### Hanoi decides on a general offensive
*6 Jul 1967 – Jan 1968 · Hanoi and COSVN · changed from history · EV000-37*
In April 1967 COSVN under General Nguyen Chi Thanh proposed a decisive general offensive in the South, and in May sent the plan to Hanoi. Thanh briefed the Politburo in July and died there on 6 July, by the most common account of a heart attack after a party, by another of wounds from a US bombing raid. The plan was carried forward by the militants around Le Duan and Le Duc Tho; General Vo Nguyen Giap, who had favoured protracted war, did not originate it and modified it reluctantly. Beginning on 27 July, hundreds of moderates, officers and intellectuals were arrested in the "Revisionist Anti-Party Affair", with a second wave in November and December, which removed opposition to the offensive and much of Giap's staff⁸⁰.

The premise was that the ARVN was no longer effective and the Saigon government unpopular, and that a general offensive in the cities would set off a general uprising. In October the Politburo chose the Tet holiday of 1968 as the date; it reaffirmed the decision in December and formalized it as Resolution 14 in January 1968⁸⁰. The border battles of the autumn, at Con Thien, Loc Ninh, Song Be and Dak To, were part of the preparation, drawing US forces away from the cities⁷¹. This was a decision taken in Hanoi for Hanoi's reasons: the stalemate in the South, the factional struggle in the Party and a reading of the South that proved wrong.

In this war the decision is taken on the same dates by the same men, and for the same reasons; the provisions did not make Le Duan a militant or remove Giap's staff. They added an argument to the militants' case and subtracted one from its premise. COSVN's reports of 1966–67 recorded that the Front's agricultural tax in the Delta was falling, that recruitment in the titled districts along the Bassac was harder each season, and that the "land trick" had not been exposed as the cadres had promised; Thanh's plan cited the erosion of the base as a reason not to wait. At the same time the premise that the Saigon government was unpopular in the countryside was weaker in the Delta than anywhere else, and COSVN's own appreciation for Military Region 9 in the autumn said so; it was overruled². The urgency rose and the forecast of a general uprising was carried forward unchanged, so that in this war the offensive was planned on a reading of the South that was more wrong in the Delta than the historical one.

The premise about the ARVN was wrong in a second way here, and the purge made sure nobody in Hanoi said so. In the spring of 1967 a study group of the General Staff's combat research department, working for Giap from the B2 and B3 Fronts' after-action reports since 1964, had concluded that the puppet infantry and its territorials, "re-equipped from the first year with American automatic weapons of a new type", no longer broke in close combat as they had in 1963 and that the local forces could not expect to overrun district towns defended by them without main-force support. The group's three senior officers were among those arrested in the second wave in December, for reasons that had nothing to do with their study and everything to do with whose staff they were on, and the study went into the files with them²¹. The offensive was planned, as historically, on the assumption that the ARVN would dissolve on contact. In this war it had been better armed than its attackers for four years.

#### The Phu Cat lot
*14 Aug – 20 Dec 1967 · A firing position of the 22nd Division's artillery on Route 1 south of Phu Cat, Binh Dinh Province; the Bien Hoa arsenal; the Special Court for Corruption, Saigon · new · EV008-5*
*Initiative: neither*

On the morning of 14 August 1967 an M101A1 105mm howitzer of a 22nd Division battery firing in support of a sweep of the Phu Cat hills burst its tube on the eleventh round of a mission. Four of the crew were killed and three wounded. The round was high explosive from a lot the Bien Hoa arsenal had forged and loaded in May, and within a week the 22nd Division's artillery had found two more rounds of the same lot with cracks at the base of the body that a gauge would have caught¹⁴.

The investigation was the Commission's, and it was quick because the paper was good. Truong Vinh Khoa's inspectors found that the lot had been accepted by a Directorate inspector on the strength of the forging line's own records, without the proof-firing of a sample that the specification required; the inspector had accepted four lots that way since March, and had been paid for the time he saved the line. He was convicted by the Special Court in December and sentenced to six years. The arsenal recalled about 40,000 rounds from five lots and destroyed about 9,000 of them¹⁴.

The gunners' verdict took longer to reverse than the court's. Through the autumn of 1967 and into 1968 the artillery of II Corps, and much of the rest of the army's, refused domestic 105mm rounds where it could draw American, and the share of 105mm bodies bought under the Aid Compliance Board's rule fell from about a third to about a tenth for most of a year. What restored it was the officers the 1964 courses had made: the Ordnance Directorate's acceptance branch, under captains and majors who could read a metallurgical report, rewrote the procedure so that every lot was proof-fired at the Long Thanh range with an officer's signature on the result, and the 22nd Division's own artillery commander was invited to witness the first ten¹⁴. Binh Dinh's gunners went back to Bien Hoa's rounds in the summer of 1968. It was a small event, and the reason it is in the record is that it showed, three years before the palace took the Directorate, what an arsenal is worth without honest acceptance: less than nothing, because a soldier who does not trust his round will not fire it.

#### The constitution and the presidential election
*3 Sep 1967 · Republic of Vietnam · historical · EV000-38*
The Constituent Assembly elected in September 1966 produced a constitution, promulgated on 1 April 1967. In the presidential election of 3 September 1967, held with the Senate election, Thieu ran for president with Ky as his running mate, after the generals forced Ky to stand aside. Eleven tickets ran. Thieu and Ky won 34.83 percent, 1,649,561 votes; the runner-up, Truong Dinh Dzu, a lawyer campaigning on negotiations with the NLF, won 17.26 percent, 817,120 votes. The reported turnout was 83.17 percent of 5,853,251 registered voters. There were credible reports of soldiers showing voters how to mark their ballots, and of a secret military committee that would govern behind the elected government; on 10 August 57 members of the US House had condemned the conduct of the campaign. Dzu was later imprisoned. Thieu was inaugurated on 31 October and the lower house was elected on 22 October⁸¹.

The election gave the Republic of Vietnam an elected government for the first time since 1963 and, under Thieu, the stability that the previous four years had lacked. It did not give it legitimacy in the countryside: two-thirds of the voters chose someone else, and a civilian peace candidate had come second.

#### The empty beds of Dinh Tuong
*4 Sep – 14 Nov 1967 · Cai Lay, Cai Be and Giao Duc Districts, Dinh Tuong Province; Memot, Kampong Cham Province, Cambodia · new · EV014-5*
*Initiative: both*

This is the operation that taught the command what the unit had actually done, and it did so by failing for ten weeks.

In the first week of September 1967 the National Fusion Centre gave the Can Tho squadron twenty-six addresses in the three upper districts of Dinh Tuong: the province committee's five surviving members, the finance section's chief, and twenty of the collectors and couriers who had been named in the ledgers taken in 1966. The file was the best in the Delta. The province had a post in every village the government held, a resident constable in most of them, a family census three years deep and a land register that said who tilled what. Between 4 September and 14 November the squadron mounted twenty-three raids on those addresses, most of them inside two hours of the decision and all at night.

It took two. A courier of nineteen was lifted out of a house in Cai Be on 19 September with a bag of receipts, and a village committee secretary at Giao Duc on 2 October. The other twenty-one raids found a bed that had been slept in and a family that said, truthfully, that the man had not been there for months.

The courier told them why, and the task force's interrogators had it out of her in two days. The province committee had not lived in Dinh Tuong since March. It lived at Memot, in the rubber across the Cambodian border, with the region's finance section, and it came into the province for four or five nights every second month, moving every night, sleeping in a different hamlet each time and never in a house named in a ledger. The rule had come down in a COSVN circular in April 1967, over Pham Hung's name — he had taken the Central Office when Nguyen Chi Thanh died in Hanoi in July, and the circular was Thanh's last instruction carried out by his successor⁸². It had three sentences in it: senior cadre do not sleep twice in a hamlet; committees meet in the base areas or across the border and never in villages; couriers carry nothing written.

At Tan Son Nhut in November the argument ran the way arguments about metrics run. The unit's raid yield in the Delta had fallen by about two-fifths from 1966 and the natural reading was that the squadron had gone slack. Vance's J-2 and the task force's analysts read it the other way and were right: the committees of the upper Delta had been driven out of the province they administered, were running it by courier from across an international border at two months' remove, and the falling yield was the measure of that success and not of failure. The Front paid for it in the only currency it had to pay in. The province's collections from the posted villages ran at about a sixth of 1964's through 1967, its rice procurement in the three districts fell with them, and the committee that had once set a village's tax rate in person now learned of a bad harvest six weeks after it happened³². It did not pay in people. The adaptation cost the Front a courier's walking time, which against 1,310 Americans and the best three hundred operators in their army is the cheapest answer any provision in this record has drawn from the enemy.

The command changed its measure in December 1967. Delta's reporting after that gave the raid count, and beside it the Front's collections in the raided districts and the distance the committees lived from them.

#### The barrier and Muscle Shoals
*8 Sep – Dec 1967 · The DMZ and the Laotian panhandle; Nakhon Phanom, Thailand · no longer happens · EV000-39*
*Consolidated at CP02 into EVCP02-4; the event still happens.*

#### The battle of Dak To
*3–23 Nov 1967 · Dak To and Hills 1338, 724 and 875, Kontum Province · no longer happens · EV000-40*
*Consolidated at CP02 into EVCP02-3; the event still happens.*

#### How the phase ends
At the end of 1967 the United States has about 486,000 military personnel in Vietnam and has lost 9,378 of them killed by hostile action in the year¹. Rolling Thunder has dropped about 864,000 tons on North Vietnam by the Pentagon's announcement of 31 December⁴⁴, and the North's air defences have made every strike near Hanoi expensive. The Trail is carrying more than ever; truck movements rise sharply from October to December. The enemy order of battle is in dispute: MACV counts about 300,000 communist personnel, the CIA up to 430,000⁸⁰, and the disagreement turns on whether the guerrillas and the political infrastructure, the part of the enemy the main-force war does not touch, are counted at all.

On 21 November Westmoreland tells the National Press Club that the enemy is "unable to mount a major offensive" and that "the end begins to come into view"⁸⁰. The administration's progress campaign of the autumn rests on the body count, the Hamlet Evaluation System and the claim that the crossover point is near. McNamara, who has lost confidence in the war, has announced his departure for the World Bank, and Johnson's offer at San Antonio on 29 September, to stop the bombing in exchange for prompt and productive talks, has been rejected by Hanoi. In Saigon there is, for the first time since 1963, an elected government under Thieu, and in the countryside CORDS has begun to organize the territorial war, from a less low base than history's. About 820,000 hectares have been titled to about 640,000 families, rent has ended in law and largely in fact wherever the government holds the night, and the Commission has convicted a general. The infrastructure of the Front is smaller in the Delta than on the historical course, and its tax take lower, but it is intact in every hamlet the government does not hold after dark, and those hamlets are still most of the contested countryside².

In Hanoi the decision is taken. The PAVN and VC main forces have been drawn to the borders and have drawn the Americans after them; the VC's local forces and cadres in and around the cities are preparing; and the Politburo has set the Tet holiday of 1968 as the moment for the General Offensive and General Uprising. The phase ends with each side certain that the other is weaker than it is, and with the population of the South, the object of the war, still largely outside the reach of either army's strategy.

In the held belt the resident infrastructure has been driven out of most villages with settled posts by the end of 1967, and the arrest count has fallen because there is little left there to arrest³. The National Police numbers about 80,000, the chiefs have mostly held their districts for two or three years, and the Front's legal cadres are in the towns, where the police knows less.

The enemy's external supply is measured: the Trail by the panhandle watch, Sihanoukville by the count, the North's roads by the Gulf watch, and every measure says that what arrives exceeds what the South's forces need from the North¹⁹. That knowledge is honest from 1965 rather than from 1970, and it has not changed the rules, the bombing or the strategy. Its main effect on Washington has been to deliver bad news early.

The RVNAF enters 1968 with the rifles it has carried for four years and a Regional Force that the corps commanders want for their own operations, bound since 1967 by the rule that keeps it in its provinces. The American army has had no rifle crisis and no inquiry, and has lost somewhat fewer men in the first minutes of its fights; it is the same army in every other respect, fighting the same battles on the enemy's terms.

The bombers have learned two things in two years: that a bridge they can measure dies in a night and is replaced by five crossings they cannot, and that a siege they can measure becomes the enemy's worst battle. The second lesson is the one the headquarters remembers going into 1968.

Phung Hoang has a centre in every district and an audited count of about 18,000 neutralized since 1965, about one in five of them members of district or village committees, and the Front's district administration in the held belt now governs from the contested edge by courier. The word Phoenix has been a press story since March 1967. About 7,000 selected advisers hold every field post below corps; the American battalions have changed over once or twice each as units, have fought better in the contacts they were granted, and have learned at Dien Ban to keep their reliefs off the dark of the moon. The staggered six-week overlap that followed raised the peak American strength in theater by about 15,000–25,000 men, which the expansion battalions of 1967 waited for, so that the enemy's tactic against the relief was paid for in American spaces rather than American dead¹¹. None of it has changed who chooses the battle.

The ARVN ends 1967 with about four in five of its battalions commanded by officers the board chose, on eighteen-month commands, with the captain-led battalion nearly gone and the thirty-day review feared and read; about a third fewer of its battalions have been rendered ineffective in 1966–67 than on the historical course, which no report shows because the enemy declined the fights that would have shown it¹². The 25th Division has a commander from the list and the corps have the commanders they had. The Military Security Service has its first file on the board's officers as a political factor, and COSVN has had every list the board published.

The factories end 1967 producing what the domestic share buys, with the steelworks short of power and the arsenal short of its gunners' trust after Phu Cat. Every post of the held belt has a radio, the Front attacks those that can call about a third less often than those that cannot, and where the guns can reach the posts the first call is for fire¹⁴. The posts hold themselves better than the hamlets around them.

The ARVN ends 1967 trained to the man; training alone would have left it as short of squad leaders as history's, and what filled most of the empty slots was the NCO schooling described below; its battalions have lost about a tenth to a sixth fewer men in the ambushes the enemy still lays, beyond what the rifles and the officers saved, and about one in eight fewer battalions has been broken in 1966–67 than the officer reforms alone would have left. The drill-sergeant corps has begun to return about 650–1,800 platoon sergeants a year to the battalions¹⁵. In the lowlands of Quang Nam and Quang Ngai the mine has replaced the firefight as the enemy's way of killing Americans, and in the districts around Saigon the Front's guerrilla squads are drilled by men the Republic trained.

The ARVN ends 1967 with its squads and platoons led by schooled men: about two in three of the slots the field commissions emptied are held by corporals first class who passed the leader's course, and the squads of the 7th Division held their base of fire at Hau My when their officers fell. I judge that about one in six to one in five fewer ARVN battalions were broken in 1966–67 than the officer reforms and the basic course alone would have left, and that the posts of the Delta lost about a tenth to a fifth fewer to night attack than the radio and the trained platoon had already saved⁶. None of it shows in a return: the enemy attacked less what kept its routine. American sergeants pass the Basic Leader Course at Bien Hoa or wait for it, and the waiver of 1966 has become a ninety-day deferral.

The combined command ends 1967 with one corps area under a Vietnamese commander and three under American deputies who in practice run them, a quarterly plan the corps commanders execute at their own speed, and a two-layer plan that protects its operations and insults its Vietnamese staff. The task force has broken the Front's district government of the Saigon belt from its edge: the Hoc Mon committee and the region's finance section govern from the Ho Bo woods, and the region's collections from the posted villages are about an eighth of 1964's¹⁶. On the II Corps coast it has a list it abandoned and a story the Front still tells.

Over the North the phase ends with the campaign the theater asked for and the result it predicted. In October 1966 this command reported that the northern campaign was destroying everything in the North that could be destroyed and that the North's war in the South needed none of it, and the barrier began from that report. The air defence that history's gradualism let the North build under sanctuary was built instead under fire and from China: about 120 MiGs fighting from Chinese fields, and SA-2 battalions three sites to a launcher. About 40–90 fewer American aircraft were lost over the North in 1966–67 than historically, and the Stennis hearings on the restrictions were never held⁷.

Delta ends the phase with its rate found and its limit found with it. Thirty-eight raids a month in 1966 and 44 in 1967, 297 listed cadre at district level or above in the two years, 61 of them province or region committee members, and nine finance sections taken with their ledgers; four in five of the raids in III and IV Corps, because that is where the file is. Ninety-four reconnaissance missions into the Laotian panhandle under the ambassador's limits produced the first measured coordinates under canopy in this war and changed what arrives in the South by one to four points, inside the width the phase already holds³². Fourteen operators are dead, four missing near the Ban Laboy ford, fifty-one wounded, and about a quarter of the men in the assault troops were selected after the war began, because the unit was committed whole and has nowhere to rotate to. And in the upper Delta the enemy has answered the whole provision with three sentences in a COSVN circular: senior cadre do not sleep twice in a hamlet, committees meet across the border, couriers carry nothing written. The committees of Dinh Tuong now govern their districts from Memot at two months' remove, their collections at about a sixth of 1964's, and the raid yield that measures this success is falling.

The gunships end 1967 with the clear night over the posts of the held belt taken from the Front, the monsoon night on the coast still its own, a hunter on every truck track in the panhandle that tonnage arriving barely notices, and the first Vietnamese gunship squadron flying. The ambush of the relief on the canal road is answered from the air.

### Mechanism

Three findings carry the phase. First, the post's chain, completed in Phase I, is now defended against the Front's best answer to it: the ambush of the relief. At My Phuoc Tay the Front drew the gunship away with a diversion and killed 31 men at the culvert; under Liem's fourth rule a gunship flew the road ahead of the next relief and found the 514th in the cane on 14 June. Second, the night attack moved where the gunship could not follow, and the monsoon nights of Binh Dinh in 1966 are the place that shows it, the event this memorandum narrates at length for a downstream effect rather than the provision's purpose. Third, the radar's tracks met a gun in the air over the panhandle, and the trucks died without the tonnage noticing.

The strongest positive effect shows itself here at its true weight. The posts of the held belt were seldom assaulted on clear nights by 1966; Liem's district held every post it had; the Delta's quietest months of the war so far were the south-west monsoon of 1966. I judge 4–10 points more of the South's attacked posts held across 1964–67 than without the gunships, on top of everything else the record had given them²². The perverse effect sits beside it: the PF stayed behind their wire under the aircraft, and IV Corps' night control rose by up to two points, not by the four or five the posts' survival suggested.

At the Ia Drang and Plei Me the gunships held the nights of a main-force campaign and changed its days not at all. They saved perhaps 10–20 American lives, chiefly at Albany, and taught the B3 Front to fight in cloud or in the forest. That is the main-force effect in one battle: the enemy who chooses to fight at night pays more, and he chooses less often.

The VNAF's gunships arrived in 1967 and found their constraint where the dossier placed it, in the radar technicians. The 817th's first sortie over Hiep Hoa is the beginning of the only part of this provision that survives 1973.

Regionally: **the Delta** — My Phuoc Tay and Hiep Hoa; **the coast** — the monsoon nights of Binh Dinh; **the Highlands** — Plei Me; **the Saigon belt** — NIGHT LANTERN's post on the Can Giuoc canal, now held under a Ghostrider rather than an AC-47.

Rice: in the coastal districts of Binh Dinh the eleven posts lost in November and December 1966 took the villages behind them out of the government's rice purchase for the 1967 spring harvest, as history's lost posts did; in the Delta the posts that held kept the Xa No canal's rice moving to Can Tho²².

### Enemy response and initiative

The Front's answers in this phase were cheap and clever. The diversion at Long Dinh was the Dinh Tuong provincial unit's own design, and it worked once. Military Region 5 waited for the monsoon. Group 559 moved the trucks under the trees and brought the guns a year early, and the guns killed a Ghostrider over the Ban Laboy ford. The adaptation that cost the enemy most was the guns: 12.7mm guns with the main-force battalions and 37mm and 57mm on the Trail, a few percent of the Trail's tonnage spent to keep the gunships off.

### Causal chains

**C015-2** [months] (begins in Phase II, EV008-4) The Dinh Tuong provincial unit draws the province's gunship to a diversion at Long Dinh and ambushes the relief at the culvert on 9 March 1967 → Liem's fourth rule puts a gunship over the road ahead of every night relief → on 14 June the 514th's ambush is found in the cane and destroyed, and it never ambushes that road again on a clear night → the district's RF companies move by road at night again in the dry season of 1967–68, and the rule of fire first relaxes in Cai Lay for the clear nights only, so that the district's posts have their reaction force back on the nights the gunship can see and only then²².

**C015-3** [war-shaping] (begins in Phase I, EV015-2) The wing forbids attacks below 3,500 feet after Tam Quan in December 1964 → the coast's posts are attacked on monsoon nights through 1966, and eleven fall in Hoai Nhon and Phu My while a Ghostrider orbits above them → Vinh Loc's figures send the VNAF's second gunship squadron to Nha Trang for the coast in 1967 rather than to Da Nang → from 1969 the coast of II Corps has the war's only all-weather night fire, and the 819th, not the 821st, is the squadron at Nha Trang that flies over Pleiku in 1975²².

### Downstream

The phase hands to Tet a held belt whose posts hold on clear nights and a coast whose posts do not; to Commando Hunt a gunship force three years on the Trail and an enemy whose guns are a year ahead of history's; and to Vietnamization a Vietnamese gunship squadron in being. The ripple into Phase III is IV Corps' night control and the year's American dead.

## §4.P3 Phase III — Tet and its aftermath (Jan 1968 – Dec 1968)

Phase III is not revised. Tet was decided by the attack itself and by Washington's reading of it, not by night fire over posts: the Ghostriders flew over Saigon, Long Binh and the district towns where history's AC-47s flew, with heavier guns, against attacks the record already shows breaking at the compound walls. Phase II's moved values — up to two more points of IV Corps' night control and 80–400 fewer American dead in 1967 — carry through Tet at the same size and do not change its course or the clock. The chapter's historical mention of the AC-130 Spectre at Commando Hunt's opening describes history's gunship; in this war the Ubon Ghostriders took its place.

## §4.P4 Phase IV — Vietnamization and drawdown (Jan 1969 – Dec 1971)

### Chronicle

*End of phase against the historical state: POP.night_control +1 to +3 against history (low) · POP.night_control.I_corps 0 to +1 against history (low) · POP.night_control.II_corps 0 to +2 against history (low) · POP.night_control.III_corps +1 to +3 against history (low) · POP.night_control.IV_corps +1 to +5 against history (low) · TRAIL.tonnage_arriving -10% to -2% against history (low) · FIND.vci +1 to +2.5 against history (low) · ARVN.self_sufficiency.fire_support -3 to +6 against history (low) · US.political_tolerance.public +1 to +4 against history (medium) · US.political_tolerance.congress 0 to +50 against history (low).*

Phase IV is the war of the American withdrawal. US strength peaks at about 539,000 in the first half of 1969 and falls to 474,000 at the end of that year, 336,000 at the end of 1970 and 158,000 at the end of 1971. American deaths by hostile action fall with it, from about 9,414 in 1969 to 4,221 in 1970 and 1,380 in 1971¹. The Republic of Vietnam's armed forces grow to replace them: from about 897,000 in 1969 to 968,000 in 1970 and about 1,048,000 in 1971–72, the territorial Regional and Popular Forces growing fastest⁸³. The policy has a name, Vietnamization, and a premise: that the South, given equipment, time and American air power, can hold what the Americans leave.

The shape of the phase is a race between the withdrawal and the South's improvement, run while Hanoi rebuilds. In the countryside, the years 1969–71 are the best the government ever has. The VC, gutted by the 1968 offensives, return to protracted war on a reduced scale; pacification spreads under Phoenix and the territorial forces, into a countryside where the land programme of 1964 has already done most of its work (the Land to the Tiller law of 1970 does not happen, EV000-65); and by most indicators, including the ones the enemy cannot fake, the VC's hold on the population of the Delta and the coastal lowlands weakens. On the frontiers the war goes the other way. The North rebuilds its main forces, extends the Trail and its pipeline, and after Sihanouk's fall loses Sihanoukville but gains much of eastern Cambodia. The United States uses the time it has to strike the sanctuaries it had left alone, secretly from the air over Cambodia from March 1969, on the ground in Cambodia in May–June 1970, and through the ARVN alone in Laos in February–March 1971, and Congress closes the ground option behind each attempt.

At home the war's support is gone. The Moratorium, the Cambodian incursion and Kent State, and the publication of the Pentagon Papers mark a country that wants its army home, and an army in Vietnam, after 1969, that is itself coming apart under drugs, racial conflict and attacks on its own officers, waiting to leave a war it no longer expects to win. In Hanoi, Ho Chi Minh dies in September 1969; the collective leadership around Le Duan negotiates secretly with Kissinger and, after the ARVN's defeat in Laos in 1971, decides to try once more for victory by conventional invasion.

In this war the phase follows the same course on the frontiers and in Washington. In the countryside it starts further on. The land programme has nothing left to do by 1970 but the communal lands, and about 1,150,000 hectares are titled by the end of 1971. The Commission Against Corruption, which had outlived three governments behind the American lever, loses that lever as the Americans leave, and in July 1971 Thieu puts it under a presidential inspectorate after it opens an inquiry that reaches his own circle².

The police and the provinces end the phase as the Commission does: their work below the palace goes on, and their reach upward ends as the American lever lapses. The historical gap narrows, because the Republic builds after 1968 what this war had from 1964.

The enemy's rear is counted through the whole phase. The Cambodian incursion goes in on a map five years in the making and finds the caches in days rather than weeks, though Washington still names COSVN as the objective; Commando Hunt's claims are checked every night against a radar count, and the claim that the trucks destroyed exceeded the trucks that existed never survives a season; and Lam Son 719 goes in with an honest estimate of what waits for it, and fails for the reasons it failed historically.

The bombers' precision reaches the Cambodian base areas in 1969 and has a season of targets there before COSVN's rear services own nothing in the open. Over the Trail it adds a point or two to what Commando Hunt costs the enemy. In Lam Son 719 it holds the bases where the PAVN dug, not the ones it rushed, and teaches I Corps to build its defence around American bombs that will soon go home.

The three provisions of 1964 that govern this memorandum change the phase at three points. The American battalions go home as they came, whole, so the withdrawals do not scatter short-timers through the units that stay; the breakdown of discipline comes on its historical course regardless. The national Phung Hoang file, built since 1964 to the standard of a warrant, is taken into the palace's service with the Board and the Directorate in 1970–71 and consolidated in one building in Saigon for the election. And Phoenix comes before Congress in 1971 with an audited count since 1965 that its critics do not dispute. The selected advisers stay on their eighteen-month tours as their numbers fall, and the territorials they advise are the best-advised part of the RVNAF when the Americans leave.

The officer reforms reach their limit in this phase, and it is the limit their memorandum named: the American lever. As the aid conditions are dropped with Vietnamization, the palace makes the board's general-officer list advisory in January 1970, keeps the board for everything below colonel, and in December 1970 answers a petition of colonels of the classes of 1957–59 by moving about forty of the army's best regimental and battalion commanders to staff and schools. Lam Son 719 goes into Laos weeks later with new commanders in two of the 1st Division's regiments and an Airborne brigade, and fails for the historical reasons, which were decided at corps and in Saigon. Thieu's election of 1971 is run against the background of the colonels' petition, read into the record of the Lower House. The battalions themselves are the best-led the Republic ever had.

Under the withdrawal, the Republic's own plants close the loop that Vietnamization would otherwise have left open: the American equipment handed over in 1969–71 goes into rebuild works and a signal depot that have run since 1966, and by the end of 1971 the RVNAF makes about two-fifths of its small-arms cartridges, most of its batteries and all of its wire, filled and powered from abroad. In the Delta the subsector advisers hand the call for fire to Vietnamese guns on Vietnamese sets before they leave. And the Directorate of Defence Industry goes to the palace with the Board, the list, the file and the Commission, keeping its output and losing its honesty.

The schooled sergeant meets the drawdown in both armies. In the American units that stay longest, the sergeant who enforces standards is the man the worst soldiers resent and some of them try to kill, and the schooled sergeant holds more squads together than history's without stopping the decay. In the Republic the gate on NCO promotion is the first of the army's merit rules that the palace breaks, by the National Day decree of 1 November 1970, in the same months it takes the board and the Directorate⁶. The academy survives the advisers, because it was Vietnamese from the start, and the territorial platoon leaders who learned to call fire on a grid call Vietnamese guns when the Americans go.

In this war the home front of 1969–71 has no My Lai and a smaller draft. The Moratorium and the Mobilization of the autumn of 1969 come on their dates without Seymour Hersh's story in their week, and the Peers inquiry, the Calley trial and the national argument of the spring of 1971 do not happen. A quarter of the men the boards would have called have enlisted before their notices came, the boards send about 70,000 fewer notices in 1969 and about 40,000 fewer in 1970, and about one in four of the Army's draftees in the rifle companies of the drawdown chose to be there⁸⁴. None of it changes the withdrawal's schedule, which Washington set in 1969 for its own reasons; it changes the bitterness of the years in which the schedule ran.

#### The sergeants of the drawdown
*1 Jan 1969 – 31 Dec 1971 · US Army and Marine Corps units in I, II and III Corps, especially the 23rd (Americal) Infantry Division at Chu Lai and LZ Baldy, Quang Tin and Quang Nam Provinces; the NCO academies in the United States · new · EV010-5*
*Initiative: neither*

The American NCO corps that fought the drawdown was schooled for its grades to the man and too thin to hold the army it led. The sergeants first class and master sergeants of 1969 were on their second or third tours; the staff sergeants and sergeants of the rifle companies were men of two or three years' service, many of them graduates of the NCO Candidate Course, who had taken the Basic Leader Course's lanes as its last module or at Bien Hoa⁶. In the units that stayed longest, the war they were asked to lead was one their soldiers had stopped believing in. Drug use, racial violence and refusals rose through 1969–71 on their historical course, and the Army and the Marine Corps recorded about 600–800 fraggings and attempted fraggings in those three years, most of them against officers and NCOs⁸⁵.

The schooled sergeant was both the man who held a squad together and the man its worst soldiers resented most. On 14 March 1970 Staff Sergeant Raymond L. Ochoa, a platoon sergeant of a battalion of the 196th Infantry Brigade at LZ Baldy, who had passed the Advanced Leader Course and had used its counselling and its standards to take four men of his platoon off the line for heroin, was wounded by a fragmentation grenade rolled under his bunk; he lived, and two of the four were convicted. The 23rd Division's inspector general counted in 1970 that the platoons whose sergeants held the course and had led them for six months or more had about a third fewer drug cases and refusals than those whose sergeants had been waived or deferred. That is the provision at its true weight: it held some squads together, and it did not stop the decay, which came from the war's politics and the draft rather than from the sergeant. The MACV academy at Bien Hoa graduated its last class on 30 June 1971 and was handed to the ARVN as a division school⁶.

In this war the draft half of that sentence was smaller. A quarter of the men the boards would have called had enlisted before their notices came, and in a rifle company of the 196th Brigade in 1970, of about 40–60 men who were draftees on the historical course, about 10–15 had chosen to come. The division's inspector general, asked in 1971 to break the 1970 figures down by how men had entered the Army, found the volunteers among the former draftees' cohort about as likely as the volunteers of every year to be on the drug and refusal lists, and about half as likely as the men still drafted; in the platoons that had a schooled sergeant, a battalion that had trained and deployed together and three or four such volunteers, he counted a further tenth to a fifth fewer cases than in Ochoa's platoon⁸⁴. It changed no fragging that the record names and saved no squad that the war's politics had already lost. The units that went home in 1971 went home as history's did, from a war their soldiers knew was being given up.

#### The Cambodian folders
*1 Jan 1969 – 30 Jun 1970 · Base Areas 350, 351, 352, 353, 609 and 740, the Fishhook and the Parrot's Beak; Memot and Snoul, Kampong Cham Province; Kratie · new · EV014-7*
*Initiative: allied*

American teams could enter Cambodia from September 1967, on foot, without tactical air, without a forward air controller overhead and with no exploitation force behind them, and those limits held until the incursion. Delta put teams across on those terms from 12 September 1967 and ran 61 missions into the base areas in 1969 beside SOG's 454⁶⁴. The teams walked in from the border at night, lay up under the rubber by day, and counted: ninety-four cache sites, thirty-one of them under permanent roofing, eleven field hospitals, four vehicle parks and the Memot headquarters complex's outer security, which was three concentric belts of bunkers manned by a battalion³². The Global Hawk's radar had been counting the road traffic from Sihanoukville to those same base areas since 1965 and could not see a cache under rubber. The folders the teams built are what the incursion's forces worked from on 29 April 1970, and they are part of why the caches fell in days rather than weeks.

COSVN was never in them. The Central Office was about 2,400 people who moved between bunkers and meeting houses by bicycle, warned of B-52 raids from 1968 by Soviet trawlers reporting the bombers' course, and it went northwest to Kratie on 19 March 1970, six weeks before the incursion crossed the border⁸². Delta's teams watched the Memot complex for eleven nights in February 1970 and reported what the complex was: a rear-service and signals installation whose senior occupants had left. The provision names COSVN as a mission; the constraint set declined it from the first day and the enemy's own mobility declined it again.

#### Nixon takes office; Vietnamization
*20 Jan – Mar 1969 · Washington and Saigon · historical · EV000-56*
Richard Nixon was inaugurated on 20 January 1969 with a promise to end the war with honour and no published plan to do it. His national security adviser, Henry Kissinger, commissioned a survey of the agencies' views of the war, which showed deep disagreement about almost everything except that the South could not yet stand alone. At a National Security Council meeting on 28 January the Secretary of Defense, Melvin Laird, proposed calling the policy "Vietnamizing" the war; its two parts were to strengthen the RVNAF to take over the fighting and to extend pacification. Henry Cabot Lodge replaced Harriman in Paris⁸⁶. Laird, who visited Saigon in March, pressed for withdrawals sooner and larger than the military wanted, and made "Vietnamization" the measure of the South's progress⁸⁷. The Improvement and Modernization programme behind it re-equipped the RVNAF from top to bottom: M16 rifles for the regulars and then the territorial forces, M60 machine guns, artillery, helicopters, aircraft, and the training base to use them.

#### The post-Tet offensive
*23 Feb – Mar 1969 · Saigon, Da Nang and installations throughout the South · historical · EV000-57*
On the night of 22–23 February 1969 the PAVN and VC opened a countrywide offensive of attacks by fire and sapper raids against more than a hundred targets, including Saigon, Da Nang, Hue and US bases, the heaviest since 1968. It was smaller in scale and ambition than Tet: the ground attacks were mainly against bases and firebases, not into the cities, and the population did not rise. It cost the Americans heavily in the first weeks, and it was taken in Washington as a breach of the understanding that had accompanied the bombing halt. Nixon's answer, in the third week of March, was the secret bombing of the Cambodian base areas⁸⁸.

#### Operation Menu
*18 Mar 1969 – 26 May 1970 · Base Areas 350, 351, 352, 353, 609 and 740, eastern Cambodia · changed from history · EV000-58*
*Initiative: allied*

On the night of 18 March 1969, 60 B-52s took off from Guam on a mission ostensibly against targets in South Vietnam; 48 were diverted into Cambodia against Base Area 353 in the Fishhook, where COSVN was believed to be, and dropped about 2,400 tons that night. In this war the 48 were Js, and their unguided carpet was half as dense; about 1,600 tons fell in the boxes, and twelve of the aircraft carried 240 GBU-31s aimed at the coordinates the U-2S had measured over five years in the Fishhook: storage sheds, a vehicle park, a workshop with a generator, and the clearings where the Global Hawk had seen trucks unload by night. The campaign that followed, codenamed Breakfast, Lunch, Snack, Dinner, Supper and Dessert after the base areas it struck, flew about 3,800 B-52 sorties and dropped 108,823 tons until 26 May 1970. Here it dropped about 75,000 tons, a fifth of the sorties carrying JDAM against measured structures²⁴. It was concealed by dual reporting: an overt channel ordered strikes near the border inside South Vietnam, while a back channel ordered the strikes in Cambodia, whose records were destroyed after each mission; neither the Secretary of the Air Force nor its chief of staff was told⁸⁸.

The measured structures died; the base areas did not. In the first six weeks of Breakfast and Lunch about 300 structures that the U-2S had photographed were destroyed, most of them sheds and workshops in clearings. By June COSVN's rear services had moved everything that mattered under the trees and underground, and the clearings were empty when the U-2S next imaged them; the J's precision had a season's targets and then none. The rear services cadres, by the account of a COSVN logistics officer who rallied in 1970, blamed spies in the base areas and searched for beacons in the forest; they found neither, and did the right thing anyway, which was to own nothing that stood in the open. COSVN itself, a mobile headquarters of a few hundred people under the canopy, was never located and never struck, as it was not historically²⁴.

Sihanouk, whose territory it was and whose government had tolerated the base areas and the supply line from Sihanoukville, did not protest publicly. The *New York Times* reported the bombing on 9 May 1969; Nixon ordered the leak traced, and the telephones of 13 officials and four journalists were tapped. Historically those wiretaps were the first of the abuses that led to Watergate; in this war there is no Watergate, the burglary of 1972 never happens, and the wiretaps of 1969 are never exposed by an investigation of it⁸⁴. The bombing forced the PAVN and VC to disperse and camouflage their Cambodian bases, here a few months earlier and more completely, but did not stop their offensives, and its effect on the war in the South is disputed⁸⁸²⁴.

#### Hamburger Hill
*13–20 May 1969 · Ap Bia Mountain (Hill 937), A Shau valley, Thua Thien Province · historical · EV000-59*
In May 1969 the 3rd Brigade of the 101st Airborne Division (Airmobile), under the division commander Major General Melvin Zais, went into the A Shau valley in Operation Apache Snow to destroy the PAVN base areas there. On Ap Bia Mountain, Hill 937, about 1.9 km from the Laotian border, the 3rd Battalion, 187th Infantry under Lieutenant Colonel Weldon Honeycutt met the 7th and 8th Battalions of the PAVN 29th Regiment, dug in. Ten days of frontal assaults followed, with the 1st Battalion, 506th Infantry, the 2nd Battalion, 501st Infantry and the ARVN 2nd Battalion, 3rd Regiment joining for the final attack on 20 May. The Americans lost 56 killed, 367 wounded and 7 missing; they counted 630 PAVN dead on and around the hill, many in tunnels, and captured 89 individual and 22 crew-served weapons and 3 prisoners. On 5 June the hill was abandoned⁸⁹.

"That hill had no military value whatsoever," Zais said. Senators Edward Kennedy, George McGovern and Stephen Young attacked the battle as senseless; on 27 June *Life* published the photographs of 242 Americans killed in one week. Abrams turned from "maximum pressure" to a posture designed to keep American casualties down, and Nixon announced the first troop withdrawal within weeks⁸⁹.

#### Midway, the first withdrawals and the Nixon Doctrine
*8 Jun – Dec 1969 · Midway Island; Guam; South Vietnam · changed from history · EV000-60*
*Initiative: allied*
On 8 June 1969 Nixon met Thieu on Midway Island and announced the first withdrawal of US troops, 25,000 men, to be completed by the end of August. Elements of the 9th Infantry Division left the Mekong Delta in July, and the whole 3rd Marine Division had left I Corps by the end of the year⁸⁷. On 25 July, at Guam, Nixon set out what became the Nixon Doctrine: the United States would honour its treaty commitments and provide a nuclear shield, but would look to its Asian allies to provide the manpower for their own defence. Further withdrawals were announced in September and December. For the troops still in Vietnam, the withdrawals made every American death harder to justify. Discipline in the US Army declined through 1969–71; drug use, racial violence and "fragging", attacks on officers and NCOs with grenades, became endemic in rear areas and in some combat units. The withdrawals were faster than the RVNAF's improvement, and they ran on a timetable set by American politics, not by the war.

In this war the withdrawals are of units, and they are cleaner for it. Under the individual system the first increment, Keystone Eagle, took the 9th Division's units home with whichever men had the least time left, and filled the units that stayed with the rest, so that the withdrawal broke up every company it touched. Here the 9th Division's battalions and the 3rd Marine Division's had each arrived as units and left as units, whole, on the dates their tours ended; no company that stayed received the short-timers of one that went, and the commanders of the battalions that stayed kept men they had trained. The cost was arithmetic: an increment had to be made of units whose tours ended in the window, so the withdrawal schedule set by Washington had to be met by shortening some tours and lengthening none, and about 12,000 men in 1969–70 went home one to four months early with their battalions. The breakdown of discipline was not prevented. Fragging, drugs and racial violence rose in 1970–71 in battalions that had trained together, as the best study of the war had led this headquarters to expect; they rose somewhat less inside the rifle companies and as much in the rear¹¹. Unit rotation preserved the unit and could not preserve the war's purpose in the minds of men who knew it was ending.

#### COSVN Resolution 9 and the death of Ho Chi Minh
*Jul – 2 Sep 1969 · COSVN headquarters and Hanoi · changed from history · EV000-61*
In July 1969 COSVN issued Resolution 9, which accepted that the 1968 offensives had not produced the uprising and directed a return to protracted war: small-unit and sapper attacks, economy of force, the rebuilding of the infrastructure, and a campaign against Vietnamization and pacification, while main forces were preserved in the sanctuaries. It was a decision taken in the light of losses and on the movement's own reading of its position, and it set the pattern of the enemy's war in the South until 1971. On 2 September 1969 Ho Chi Minh died in Hanoi. His testament called for the war to continue until the Americans had left and the country was reunified. Power passed to a collective leadership in which Le Duan, as First Secretary, was the strongest figure, with Le Duc Tho, Truong Chinh and Pham Van Dong. Ho's death did not change the policy; it removed the one figure whose authority stood above the factions, and left the militants who had planned Tet in charge.

In this war Resolution 9 is issued on the same date with the same direction. Its section on the countryside is different in one respect. On the historical course the Front could still campaign in 1969 against a government that had not given the peasants their land. Here the government had been titling since 1964, and the resolution's instruction to the Delta's party committees was to stop contesting the titles, to treat a titled hamlet the government held at night as lost for the present, and to concentrate cadres and the reduced tax effort on the hamlets the government held only by day. That was a realistic instruction and a costly admission: it surrendered the issue on which the Front had been built in the Delta, and it left the movement there with nothing to offer the peasant but reunification and the end of the American war².

The resolution's instruction on the infrastructure was the Front's admission of what the police had done to it. It told the party committees of the Delta and the provinces around Saigon to stop keeping cadres resident in villages with settled police posts, to govern them by visiting cadres who slept elsewhere, to concentrate the reduced effort on the hamlets the government held only by day, and to put the movement's best security cadres into penetration of the Special Branch and of the provincial administration rather than into the villages. A movement that had lived among its people since 1960 now visited them, and visiting cadres taxed less, recruited less and governed less³.

#### The closed loop
*1 Jul 1969 – 31 Dec 1971 · The Bien Hoa rebuild works and the Go Vap signal depot; the ARVN depots at Long Binh and Da Nang; the 1st and 3rd Area Logistics Commands · new · EV008-6*
*Initiative: allied*

Vietnamization handed the RVNAF the equipment of an American army in three years, and in this war the Republic already had the shops to keep it running. From the summer of 1969 the departing American units turned in their vehicles, radios and weapons for transfer, and the Bien Hoa rebuild works and the Go Vap signal depot, which had rebuilt the RVNAF's own engines, carriers and sets at the domestic share since 1966, took the American articles into the same loop: in 1970 the works rebuilt about 4,500 engines, 2,000 transmissions and about 550 M113s and M41s, and the depot about 45,000 radios, most of them PRC-25s and PRC-77s, which the communications plant had built under licence since 1969. The crash depot programme that the historical RVNAF needed in 1970–71, when it found itself owning more equipment than it could repair, did not have to be built, and the American logistics staff that would have built it spent the time on the ports¹³¹⁴.

The loop was tested in the spring of 1971. Lam Son 719 lost about a third of the 1st Armored Brigade's carriers and tanks in Laos, most of them abandoned on Route 9 where no rebuild works could reach them. Of those that came back damaged, the works at Da Nang and Bien Hoa returned about 60 to the brigade by August, and its squadrons were at about four-fifths of their establishment again by the end of the year¹⁴. The brigade's losses in Laos were history's, and the reason was the operation, not the depot. What the depot changed was how long it took to stand the brigade up again.

By the end of 1971 about two-fifths of the RVNAF's small-arms cartridge cases, about a third of its 105mm bodies, most of its mortar bodies, most of its radio batteries and all of its field wire came from its own plants, filled and powered with American propellant, explosive and semiconductors bought with aid dollars. It was the largest step toward a self-supplying army the Republic ever took, and in 1971 nobody could see it, because the American stocks were still free and still flowing. The Joint General Staff's J-4, which in 1970 would historically have been absorbed by the turnover, spent part of that year drafting the first plan for an RVNAF that would buy its own ammunition with its own money; it was filed and not funded¹⁴.

#### ◆ The night net changes hands
*1 Jul 1969 – 31 Dec 1971 · Bien Hoa, Binh Thuy, Nha Trang and Da Nang air bases; the posts of the four corps · new · EV015-6*
*Initiative: allied*

The Ghostriders went home on Vietnamization's schedule, and the night over the posts passed to the 81st Gunship Wing in stages. The 4th Air Commando Squadron left Bien Hoa in October 1970, the 14th left Nha Trang in December 1971, and the 16th at Da Nang and the 17th at Ubon stayed into 1972. The 817th Squadron took the night over III and IV Corps from October 1970 with a detachment of five aircraft at Binh Thuy for the lower Delta; the 819th at Nha Trang held the coast of II Corps from the spring of 1969; and the 821st at Da Nang reached strength at the end of 1969 and shared I Corps with the 16th²².

At its best, in 1970–71, the wing flew about 700–850 sorties a month at about 55% of aircraft operational, against the Ghostriders' 1,300–1,500 at the peak of 1967–68. It covered about two-thirds of the posts the Ghostriders had covered, the radar beacons on the district towns and the most-attacked posts gave it the coast's monsoon nights the Ghostriders never had, and its answers came in Vietnamese. In the north-east monsoon of 1969–70 the 819th answered about 60% of the posts' calls in Binh Dinh and Phu Yen on cloudy nights, against almost none in history's coast and few in this war's before 1968. The posts it did not cover — the islands of An Xuyen and Bac Lieu beyond Binh Thuy's reach, the Highland posts of Kontum and Darlac — were attacked more often as the Americans left, and held about as history's did²².

The wing lost four aircraft in the three years: one to 12.7mm fire over Kien Phong in March 1970, one to a 37mm battery over Cambodia in August 1970, one in a landing accident at Nha Trang in the monsoon of 1970, and one to a mortar on the ramp at Da Nang in 1971. Its crews were the best-educated men in the VNAF and the most often sought by the airlines; about one in six left the wing in 1971, and the radar technicians, the scarcest of them, were held by an order of the Joint General Staff that barred their transfer²².

#### The secret Paris channel opens
*4 Aug 1969 – Feb 1970 · Paris · historical · EV000-62*
In June 1969 the National Liberation Front formed the Provisional Revolutionary Government of the Republic of South Vietnam, to claim governmental status at the talks. On 4 August Kissinger began secret talks in Paris with Hanoi's delegation, first with Xuan Thuy, and from February 1970 with Le Duc Tho, a member of the Politburo. The formal sessions at the Hotel Majestic became a forum for public statements; the substance moved to the secret channel. The core issues were fixed from the start and remained so for three years: Hanoi demanded the withdrawal of all US forces and the removal of Thieu as the price of a settlement, and would not discuss the withdrawal of its own forces from the South; Washington would not overthrow its ally⁸⁶.

#### The Moratorium; no My Lai to reveal
*15 Oct – 24 Nov 1969 · Washington and the United States · changed from history · EV000-63*
*Initiative: neither*
On 15 October 1969 the Moratorium brought hundreds of thousands of Americans out against the war in cities across the country, and on 15 November the Mobilization brought a march of several hundred thousand to Washington. On 3 November Nixon appealed on television to "the great silent majority" to support his policy of withdrawal on his terms, and the speech was well received. Historically, on 13 November Seymour Hersh published the story of the My Lai massacre, the photographs taken by the Army photographer Ronald Haeberle appeared on 20 November, and on 24 November the Army appointed Lieutenant General William Peers to investigate the cover-up⁹⁰.

In this war there was nothing to publish. No massacre had been committed at Son My on 16 March 1968, Ridenhour had written no letters, and Haeberle's photographs of Task Force Barker's sweep showed soldiers searching empty bunkers. The Mobilization of 15 November marched in a week whose news was the President's speech and the first troop withdrawals, not a massacre. The antiwar movement's case was the war itself, the casualty lists of 1969 at about 9,400 American dead¹ and the lack of an end, and it did not need My Lai to make it; it lost the war's single most powerful image of what the fighting did to the villages, and its moral argument was weaker for the loss while its political argument was untouched. In the press the incidents that history overshadowed with My Lai, the free-fire areas of Quang Ngai and the refugee camps on Route 1, were reported as they were found, one at a time and smaller. No Peers inquiry was appointed. The Army was not made to look at the command climate of the Americal Division, and MACV's Inspector General's proposal of 1970 for a theater-wide inquiry into how the body count shaped the conduct of operations was not taken up in Washington, because without a scandal no one above MACV wanted it⁸⁴. Gallup's share of Americans calling the war a mistake had been 53% in August 1968 and averaged about 55% in 1969–70⁹¹; that verdict was in before November 1969, and it was the verdict the President's withdrawal answered.

#### The Board and the Directorate in the palace
*Jan 1970 – Sep 1971 · Saigon and the provinces · new · EV002-6*
*Initiative: allied*
The Provincial Administration Selection Board had outlived four governments because each had found it cheaper to keep than to break, and because a province chief appointed outside the list could not sign a budget, a title or a counterpart-fund disbursement while the Aid Compliance Board stood behind the rule⁹. By 1970 the American lever was lapsing with the withdrawal, and Thieu, preparing for the election of 1971, wanted the provinces. He did not abolish the Board. As the terms of its two general officers expired in January 1970 he replaced them with officers of his own staff, and from that spring his Prime Minister, General Tran Thien Khiem, appointed the third name on the list for about half the province vacancies and for most of those in the provinces with the largest electorates. The list still excluded the incompetent and the bought; it no longer excluded the loyal mediocre³.

The Inspectorate's reports, which had gone to the Board and the Commission since 1964, were routed through the presidential Inspectorate General from July 1971 under the decree that subordinated the Commission. The National Police had followed the same road earlier: its Director General had been the President's appointee in fact since 1966, and in 1970–71 the Special Branch's Saigon headquarters spent a growing share of its effort on the opposition, the An Quang Buddhists and the Ky camp, as historical police chiefs always had. The rural posts, the census and the district sections went on much as before, because they were too many and too local for the palace to want them. In September 1971 province chiefs campaigned for Thieu as they did on the historical course; the difference was that about two-thirds of them were men who had passed an examination and served a district first, and the villages they governed were governed better than they were represented³.

The palace took Phung Hoang's national file the same way, without abolishing anything. In February 1970 Khiem, who was Minister of the Interior as well as Prime Minister, moved the national centre from the Directorate of National Police to the Central Intelligence Organization's building and replaced Hieu, who resigned rather than serve under the new chairman, with a Military Security colonel of the President's staff.
